and define the infinite Hankel matrix

H_{\infty}(\mathchoice{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{O}$}}_{0}(P))=((P^{n+n^{\prime}})_{00})_{n,n^{\prime}\geq 0}
.
If
P
is totally positive of order
r
, then so is
H_{\infty}(\mathchoice{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{O}$}}_{0}(P))
.

Once again we can we can improve these results
to replace
P
by
aI+bP
.
It suffices to note that

H_{\infty}(\mathchoice{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{O}$}}_{0}(aI+bP))&={\mathcal{O}}(aI+bP)\,{{\mathcal{O}}(aI+bP^{\rm T})}^{\rm T}\\
=B_{a,b}\,{\mathcal{O}}(P)\,{{\mathcal{O}}(P^{\rm T})}^{\rm T}\,(B_{a,b})^{\rm T}\\
=B_{a,b}\,H_{\infty}(\mathchoice{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{O}$}}_{0}(P))\,(B_{a,b})^{\rm T}

(2.23)

by Lemmas
2.11
and
2.13
.
Since the binomial matrix
B_{a,b}
is totally positive
by Lemma
2.2
, we conclude:

Corollary 2.16

(Hankel-total positivity of zeroth column, improved)
.

Let
P
be an infinite matrix that is either row-finite or column-finite,
with entries in a partially ordered commutative ring
R
.
Then
P
is TP
r

\implies

{\mathcal{O}}(P)
and
{\mathcal{O}}(P^{\rm T})
are TP
r

\implies

\mathchoice{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{O}$}}_{0}(P)
is Hankel-TP
r

\iff

\mathchoice{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{O}$}}_{0}(aI+bP)
is Hankel-TP
r
for all
a,b\geq 0
.

Once again,
a
and
b
can be nonnegative elements of
R
,
or
a
and
b
can be indeterminates
and we work in the ring
R[a,b]
equipped with the coefficientwise order.
Corollary
2.16
allows us to prove the
Hankel-total positivity of the output sequence
{\mathchoice{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{O}$}}_{0}(aI+bP)}

whenever
P
is TP, even if
aI+bP
is not TP.
It will play an important role in our proof of Theorem
1.4
.

Remark.

We see from (
) specialized to
k=0

that
\mathchoice{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{O}$}}_{0}(aI+bP)
is a binomial transform of
\mathchoice{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{O}$}}_{0}(P)
:

\mathchoice{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{O}$}}_{0}(aI+bP)_{n}\;=\;\sum_{j=0}^{n}a^{n-j}\,b^{j}\,\binom{n}{j}\,\mathchoice{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{O}$}}_{0}(P)_{j}\;.

(2.24)

Now, it is known
[
89
,
70
]

that the binomial transform preserves Hankel-TP
r
of arbitrary sequences
in a partially ordered commutative ring.
So Corollary
2.16
is just a special case
of this general result combined with Theorem
2.15
.

\blacksquare

2.4
Binomial row-generating matrices

Let
A=(a_{nk})_{n,k\geq 0}
be a row-finite matrix
with entries in a commutative ring
R
.
(In most applications, including all those in the present paper,
the matrix
A
will be lower-triangular.)
We define its
row-generating polynomials
in the usual way:

A_{n}(x)\;\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\rm def}}{{=}}\;\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}a_{nk}\,x^{k}\;,

(2.25)

where the sum is actually finite because
A
is row-finite.
More generally, let us define its

binomial partial row-generating polynomials

A_{n,k}(x)&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\rm def}}{{=}}\sum_{\ell=k}^{\infty}a_{n\ell}\,\binom{\ell}{k}\,x^{\ell-k}\\
={1\over k!}\,{d^{k}\over dx^{k}}\,A_{n}(x)\;.

(2.26)

(Note that the operator
(1/k!)\,d^{k}\!/\!dx^{k}
has a well-defined action
on the polynomial ring
R[x]
even if
R
does not contain the rationals,
since
(1/k!)(d^{k}\!/\!dx^{k})x^{n}=\binom{n}{k}x^{n-k}
.)
The polynomials
A_{n,k}(x)
are the matrix elements of the

binomial row-generating matrix

AB_{x}
:

(AB_{x})_{nk}\;=\;A_{n,k}(x)\;,

(2.27)

where
B_{x}=B_{x,1}
is the weighted binomial matrix defined in (
1.13
).
The zeroth column of the matrix
AB_{x}
consists
of the row-generating polynomials
A_{n}(x)=A_{n,0}(x)
.

In this paper the matrix
A

will be either the Laguerre coefficient matrix
{\sf L}^{(\alpha)}

or one of its multivariate generalizations.

We can now explain the method that we will use to prove
Theorems
1.1
and
1.4
:

Proposition 2.17
.

Let
P
be a row-finite matrix
with entries in a partially ordered commutative ring
R
,
and let
A={\mathcal{O}}(P)
.

(a)

If
P
is totally positive of order
r
, then so is
A
.

(b)

If the matrix
B_{x}^{-1}PB_{x}
is totally positive of order
r

in the ring
R[x]
equipped with the coefficientwise order,
then the sequence
(A_{n}(x))_{n\geq 0}
of row-generating polynomials
is Hankel-totally positive of order
r

in the ring
R[x]
equipped with the coefficientwise order.

Indeed, (a) is just a restatement of Theorem
2.9
;
and (b) is an immediate consequence of Lemma
2.7

and Theorem
2.15

together with the fact that the zeroth column of the matrix
AB_{x}
consists
of the row-generating polynomials
A_{n}(x)
.

Remark.

The binomial row-generating matrix
AB_{x}

can also be considered as a modified Wronskian matrix.
To see this, let
N\in{\mathbb{N}}\cup\{\infty\}
,
and fix formal power series
f_{1}(x),\ldots,f_{N}(x)\in R[[x]]
.
We define the
N\times\infty

Wronskian matrix

W(f_{1},\ldots,f_{N})(x)\;=\;\big(f_{n}^{(k)}(x)\bigr)_{1\leq n\leq N,\,k\geq 0}

(2.28)

(where
(k)
denotes the
k
th derivative)
and the
N\times\infty

modified Wronskian matrix

\widetilde{W}(f_{1},\ldots,f_{N})(x)\;=\;\big(f_{n}^{(k)}(x)/k!\bigr)_{1\leq n\leq N,\,k\geq 0}\;;

(2.29)

these are matrices with entries in
R[[x]]
.
Of course, we have

W(f_{1},\ldots,f_{N})(x)\;=\;\widetilde{W}(f_{1},\ldots,f_{N})(x)\>D

(2.30)

where
D=\mathop{\rm diag}\nolimits\big((k!)_{k\geq 0}\big)
.
The key fact is that

\widetilde{W}(f_{1},\ldots,f_{N})(x)\;=\;\widetilde{W}(f_{1},\ldots,f_{N})(0)\>B_{x}\;.

(2.31)

In particular, if the ring
R
carries a partial order,
the total positivity of order
r

of the matrix
\widetilde{W}(f_{1},\ldots,f_{N})(0)

in the ring
R

implies the total positivity of order
r

of the matrix
\widetilde{W}(f_{1},\ldots,f_{N})(x)

in the ring
R[[x]]
equipped with the coefficientwise order.

\blacksquare

2.5
Exponential Riordan arrays

Let
R
be a commutative ring containing the rationals,
and let
F(t)=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}f_{n}t^{n}/n!

and
G(t)=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}g_{n}t^{n}/n!
be formal power series
with coefficients in
R
; we set
g_{0}=0
.
Then the
exponential Riordan array

[
24
,
23
,
5
]

associated to the pair
(F,G)

— or equivalently to the pair of sequences

{\bm{f}}=(f_{n})_{n\geq 0}
and
{\bm{g}}=(g_{n})_{n\geq 1}
—
is the infinite lower-triangular matrix

{\mathcal{R}}[F,G]=({\mathcal{R}}[F,G]_{nk})_{n,k\geq 0}
defined by

{\mathcal{R}}[F,G]_{nk}\;=\;{n!\over k!}\>[t^{n}]\,F(t)G(t)^{k}\;.

(2.32)

That is, the
k
th column of
{\mathcal{R}}[F,G]

has exponential generating function
F(t)G(t)^{k}/k!
.
It follows that the bivariate exponential generating function
of
{\mathcal{R}}[F,G]
is

\sum_{n,k=0}^{\infty}{\mathcal{R}}[F,G]_{nk}\>{t^{n}\over n!}\,u^{k}\;=\;F(t)\,e^{uG(t)}\;.

(2.33)

Please note that the diagonal elements of
{\mathcal{R}}[F,G]

are
{\mathcal{R}}[F,G]_{nn}=f_{0}g_{1}^{n}
,
so the matrix
{\mathcal{R}}[F,G]
is invertible
in the ring
R^{{\mathbb{N}}\times{\mathbb{N}}}_{\rm lt}
of lower-triangular matrices
if and only if
f_{0}
and
g_{1}
are invertible in
R
.

We shall use an easy but important result that is sometimes called
the
fundamental theorem of exponential Riordan arrays
(FTERA):

Lemma 2.18

(Fundamental theorem of exponential Riordan arrays)
.

Let
{\bm{b}}=(b_{n})_{n\geq 0}
be a sequence with
exponential generating function
B(t)=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}b_{n}t^{n}/n!
.
Considering
{\bm{b}}
as a column vector and letting
{\mathcal{R}}[F,G]

act on it by matrix multiplication, we obtain a sequence
{\mathcal{R}}[F,G]{\bm{b}}

whose exponential generating function is
F(t)\,B(G(t))
.

Proof.
We compute

\sum_{k=0}^{n}{\mathcal{R}}[F,G]_{nk}\,b_{k}&=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}{n!\over k!}\,[t^{n}]\,F(t)G(t)^{k}\,b_{k}\\
=n!\>[t^{n}]\>F(t)\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}b_{k}\,{G(t)^{k}\over k!}\\
=n!\>[t^{n}]\>F(t)\,B(G(t))\;.

(2.34)

\square

Let us now consider the product of two exponential Riordan arrays

{\mathcal{R}}[F_{1},G_{1}]
and
{\mathcal{R}}[F_{2},G_{2}]
.
Applying the FTERA to the
k
th column of
{\mathcal{R}}[F_{2},G_{2}]
,
whose exponential generating function is
F_{2}(t)G_{2}(t)^{k}/k!
,
we readily obtain:

Lemma 2.19

(Product of two exponential Riordan arrays)
.

We have

{\mathcal{R}}[F_{1},G_{1}]\,{\mathcal{R}}[F_{2},G_{2}]\;=\;{\mathcal{R}}[(F_{2}\circ G_{1})F_{1},\,G_{2}\circ G_{1}]

(2.35)

where
\circ
denotes composition of formal power series.

In particular, if we let
{\mathcal{R}}[F_{2},G_{2}]

be the weighted binomial matrix
B_{\xi}={\mathcal{R}}[e^{\xi t},t]
,
we obtain:

Corollary 2.20

(Binomial row-generating matrix of an exponential Riordan array)
.

We have

{\mathcal{R}}[F,G]\,B_{\xi}\;=\;{\mathcal{R}}[{\rm e}^{\xi G}F,G]\;.

(2.36)

We can now determine the production matrix of an exponential
Riordan array
{\mathcal{R}}[F,G]
.
Let
{\bm{a}}=(a_{n})_{n\geq 0}
and
{\bm{z}}=(z_{n})_{n\geq 0}

be sequences in a commutative ring
R
,
with ordinary generating functions

A(s)=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}a_{n}s^{n}

and
Z(s)=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}z_{n}s^{n}
.
We then define the
exponential AZ matrix

associated to the sequences
{\bm{a}}
and
{\bm{z}}

to be the lower-Hessenberg matrix with entries

\mathop{\rm EAZ}\nolimits({\bm{a}},{\bm{z}})_{nk}\;=\;{n!\over k!}\>(z_{n-k}\,+\,k\,a_{n-k+1})

(2.37)

(where
z_{-1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\rm def}}{{=}}0
),
or equivalently (if
R
contains the rationals)

\mathop{\rm EAZ}\nolimits({\bm{a}},{\bm{z}})\;=\;D\,T_{\infty}({\bm{z}})\,D^{-1}\>+\>D\,T_{\infty}({\bm{a}})\,D^{-1}\,\Delta

(2.38)

where
D=\mathop{\rm diag}\nolimits\bigl((n!)_{n\geq 0}\bigr)
.
We also write
\mathop{\rm EAZ}\nolimits(A,Z)
as a synonym for
\mathop{\rm EAZ}\nolimits({\bm{a}},{\bm{z}})
.

Theorem 2.21

(Production matrices of exponential Riordan arrays)
.

Let
L
be a lower-triangular matrix
(with entries in a commutative ring
R
containing the rationals)
with invertible diagonal entries and
L_{00}=1
,
and let
P=L^{-1}\Delta L
be its production matrix.
Then
L
is an exponential Riordan array
if and only if
P
is an exponential AZ matrix.

More precisely,
L={\mathcal{R}}[F,G]
if and only if
P=\mathop{\rm EAZ}\nolimits(A,Z)
,
where the generating functions
\big(F(t),G(t)\big)

and
\big(A(s),Z(s)\big)
are connected by

G^{\prime}(t)\;=\;A(G(t))\;,\qquad{F^{\prime}(t)\over F(t)}\;=\;Z(G(t))

(2.39)

or equivalently

A(s)\;=\;G^{\prime}(\bar{G}(s))\;,\qquad Z(s)\;=\;{F^{\prime}(\bar{G}(s))\over F(\bar{G}(s))}

(2.40)

where
\bar{G}(s)
is the compositional inverse of
G(t)
.

Proof

(mostly contained in
[
5
, pp. 217–218]
).
Suppose that
L={\mathcal{R}}[F,G]
.
The hypotheses on
L
imply that
f_{0}=1

and that
g_{1}
is invertible in
R
;
so
G(t)
has a compositional inverse.
Now let
P=(p_{nk})_{n,k\geq 0}
be a matrix;
its column exponential generating functions are, by definition,

P_{k}(t)=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}p_{nk}\,t^{n}/n!
.
Applying the FTERA to each column of
P
,
we see that
{\mathcal{R}}[F,G]P
is a matrix
whose column exponential generating functions
are
\big(F(t)\,P_{k}(G(t))\big)_{k\geq 0}
.
On the other hand,
\Delta\,{\mathcal{R}}[F,G]

is the matrix
{\mathcal{R}}[F,G]
with its zeroth row removed
and all other rows shifted upwards,
so it has column exponential generating functions

{d\over dt}\,\big(F(t)\,G(t)^{k}/k!\big)\;=\;{1\over k!}\>\Big[F^{\prime}(t)\,G(t)^{k}\>+\>k\,F(t)\,G(t)^{k-1}\,G^{\prime}(t)\Big]\;.

(2.41)

Comparing these two results, we see that

\Delta\,{\mathcal{R}}[F,G]={\mathcal{R}}[F,G]\,P

if and only if

P_{k}(G(t))\;=\;{1\over k!}\>{F^{\prime}(t)\,G(t)^{k}\>+\>k\,F(t)\,G(t)^{k-1}\,G^{\prime}(t)\over F(t)}\;,

(2.42)

or in other words

P_{k}(t)\;=\;{1\over k!}\>\biggl[{F^{\prime}(\bar{G}(t))\over F(\bar{G}(t))}\,t^{k}\>+\>k\,t^{k-1}\,G^{\prime}(\bar{G}(t))\biggr]\;.

(2.43)

Therefore

p_{nk}&={n!\over k!}\>[t^{n}]\,\biggl[{F^{\prime}(\bar{G}(t))\over F(\bar{G}(t))}\,t^{k}\>+\>k\,t^{k-1}\,G^{\prime}(\bar{G}(t))\biggr]\\
={n!\over k!}\>\biggl[[t^{n-k}]\>{F^{\prime}(\bar{G}(t))\over F(\bar{G}(t))}\>+\>k\,[t^{n-k+1}]\>G^{\prime}(\bar{G}(t))\biggr]\\
={n!\over k!}\>(z_{n-k}\,+\,k\,a_{n-k+1})

(2.44)

where
{\bm{a}}=(a_{n})_{n\geq 0}
and
{\bm{z}}=(z_{n})_{n\geq 0}

are given by (
2.40
).

Conversely, suppose that
P=\mathop{\rm EAZ}\nolimits(A,Z)
.
Define
F(t)
and
G(t)

as the unique solutions (in the formal-power-series ring
R[[t]]
)
of the differential equations (
2.39
)
with initial conditions
F(0)=1
and
G(0)=0
.
Then running the foregoing computation backwards
shows that
\Delta\,{\mathcal{R}}[F,G]={\mathcal{R}}[F,G]\,P
.

\square

A central role will be played later in this paper
by a simple but remarkable identity for
B_{x}^{-1}\mathop{\rm EAZ}\nolimits({\bm{a}},{\bm{z}})\,B_{x}
,
where
B_{x}
is the
x
-binomial matrix defined in (
1.13
):

Lemma 2.22

(Identity for
B_{x}^{-1}\mathop{\rm EAZ}\nolimits({\bm{a}},{\bm{z}})\,B_{x}
)
.

Let
{\bm{a}}=(a_{n})_{n\geq 0}
,
{\bm{z}}=(z_{n})_{n\geq 0}
and
x

be indeterminates.
Then

B_{x}^{-1}\mathop{\rm EAZ}\nolimits({\bm{a}},{\bm{z}})\,B_{x}\;=\;\mathop{\rm EAZ}\nolimits({\bm{a}},{\bm{z}}+x{\bm{a}})

(2.45)

as an identity in
{\mathbb{Z}}[{\bm{a}},{\bm{z}}]
.

The special case
{\bm{z}}={\bm{0}}
of this lemma was proven in

[
58
, Lemma 3.6]
;
a simpler proof was given in
[
67
, Lemma 2.16]
.
Here we give the easy generalization to include
{\bm{z}}
,
taken from
[
13
]
:

Proof.
We work temporarily in the ring
{\mathbb{Q}}[{\bm{a}},{\bm{z}}]

and use the matrix definition (
2.38
):

\mathop{\rm EAZ}\nolimits({\bm{a}},{\bm{z}})\;=\;D\,T_{\infty}({\bm{z}})\,D^{-1}\>+\>D\,T_{\infty}({\bm{a}})\,D^{-1}\,\Delta

(2.46)

where
D=\mathop{\rm diag}\nolimits\bigl((n!)_{n\geq 0}\bigr)
.
Since
\mathop{\rm EAZ}\nolimits({\bm{a}},{\bm{z}})=\mathop{\rm EAZ}\nolimits({\bm{a}},{\bm{0}})+\mathop{\rm EAZ}\nolimits({\bm{0}},{\bm{z}})
,
it suffices to consider separately the two contributions.

The key observation is that

B_{x}=D\,T_{\infty}\big((x^{n}/n!)_{n\geq 0}\big)\,D^{-1}
.
Now two Toeplitz matrices always commute:

T_{\infty}({\bm{a}})\,T_{\infty}({\bm{b}})=T_{\infty}({\bm{a}}\star{\bm{b}})=T_{\infty}({\bm{b}})\,T_{\infty}({\bm{a}})
.
It follows that
DT_{\infty}({\bm{z}})D^{-1}
and
DT_{\infty}({\bm{a}})D^{-1}

commute with
B_{x}
.
Therefore

B_{x}^{-1}\mathop{\rm EAZ}\nolimits({\bm{0}},{\bm{z}})\,B_{x}\;=\;\mathop{\rm EAZ}\nolimits({\bm{0}},{\bm{z}})\;.

(2.47)

On the other hand, the classic recurrence for binomial coefficients implies

\Delta B_{x}\;=\;B_{x}\,(xI+\Delta)

(2.48)

(cf. Example
2.10
). Therefore

B_{x}^{-1}\mathop{\rm EAZ}\nolimits({\bm{a}},{\bm{0}})B_{x}&=B_{x}^{-1}\,DT_{\infty}({\bm{a}})D^{-1}\,\Delta\,B_{x}\\
=B_{x}^{-1}\,DT_{\infty}({\bm{a}})D^{-1}\,B_{x}\,(xI+\Delta)\\
=DT_{\infty}({\bm{a}})D^{-1}\,(xI+\Delta)\\
=\mathop{\rm EAZ}\nolimits({\bm{a}},x{\bm{a}})\;.

(2.49)

Adding (
2.47
) and (
2.49
) yields (
2.45
).

\square

The identity (
2.45
) can alternatively be proven
by combining Lemma
2.7

and Corollary
2.20

with Theorem
2.21
:
see
[
13
]
for details.

3
Production matrices: Univariate case

In this section we will prove Proposition
1.2
,
which gives the production matrices
for the Laguerre coefficient matrix
{\sf L}^{(\alpha)}

[defined in (
1.12
)]
and for the binomial row-generating matrix
{\sf L}^{(\alpha)}B_{x}
.
The proofs are in fact quite easy.
We also give a simple direct proof of the coefficientwise total positivity
of the Laguerre coefficient matrix
{\sf L}^{(-1+\lambda)}
,

3.1
Coefficient matrix
{\sf L}^{\bm{(\alpha)}}
: Proof of Proposition
1.2
(a)

We begin by proving Proposition
1.2
(a),
which asserts that the production matrix
for the Laguerre coefficient matrix
{\sf L}^{(\alpha)}

is the tridiagonal unit-lower-Hessenberg matrix

P^{\circ}=(p^{\circ}_{ij})_{i,j\geq 0}
defined by

p^{\circ}_{n,n+1}&=1\\
p^{\circ}_{n,n}=2n+1+\alpha\\
p^{\circ}_{n,n-1}=n(n+\alpha)\\
p^{\circ}_{n,k}=0\qquad\textrm{if $k<n-1$ or $k>n+1$}

(3.1)

We give two proofs:
one by direct computation,
and one using the theory of exponential Riordan arrays.

First Proof.
Recall that the Laguerre coefficient matrix
{\sf L}^{(\alpha)}

has entries

\ell^{(\alpha)}_{n,k}\;\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\rm def}}{{=}}\;\binom{n}{k}\,(\alpha+1+k)^{\overline{n-k}}\;.

(3.2)

We need to verify that

\sum_{j}\ell^{(\alpha)}_{n,j}\,p^{\circ}_{j,k}=\ell^{(\alpha)}_{n+1,k}
,
or in other words that

\ell^{(\alpha)}_{n,k-1}\>+\>(2k+1+\alpha)\ell^{(\alpha)}_{n,k}\>+\>(k+1)(k+1+\alpha)\ell^{(\alpha)}_{n,k+1}\;=\;\ell^{(\alpha)}_{n+1,k}\;.

(3.3)

This is a straightforward computation.

\square

Second Proof.
It follows from (
1.10
) and (
2.33
)
that the matrix
{\sf L}^{(\alpha)}
is an exponential Riordan array
{\mathcal{R}}[F,G]

with
F(t)=(1-t)^{-(1+\alpha)}
and
G(t)=t/(1-t)
.
Since

G^{\prime}(t)\;=\;[1+G(t)]^{2}\;,

(3.4)

from (
2.39
) we have

A(s)\;=\;1+2s+s^{2}\;.

(3.5)

Then

{F^{\prime}(t)\over F(t)}\;=\;{1+\alpha\over 1-t}\;=\;(1+\alpha)\,[1+G(t)]\;,

(3.6)

so from (
2.39
) we have

Z(s)\;=\;(1+\alpha)\,(1+s)\;.

(3.7)

By Theorem
2.21
,
the production matrix for
{\sf L}^{(\alpha)}

is the exponential AZ matrix
\mathop{\rm EAZ}\nolimits(A,Z)
.
Inserting (
3.5
)/(
3.7
) into (
2.37
)
yields (
3.1
).

\square

We now wish to make an observation about the connection
of the Laguerre coefficient matrix
{\sf L}^{(\alpha)}
with continued fractions.
The zeroth column of
{\sf L}^{(\alpha)}
is the sequence
of rising powers (= Stirling cycle polynomials)

(\lambda^{\overline{n}})_{n\geq 0}
, where
\lambda\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\rm def}}{{=}}\alpha+1
.
And this sequence
has a well-known classical S-fraction for its ordinary generating function,
which was found more than two-and-a-half centuries ago by Euler

[
26
, section 26]

[
27
]
13
13

13

The paper
[
26
]
,
which is E247 in Eneström’s
[
25
]
catalogue,
was probably written circa 1746;
it was presented to the St. Petersburg Academy in 1753,
and published in 1760.
The paper
[
27
]
,
which is E616 in Eneström’s
[
25
]
catalogue,
was apparently presented to the St. Petersburg Academy in 1776,
and published posthumously in 1788.

:

\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\lambda^{\overline{n}}\>t^{n}\;=\;\cfrac{1}{1-\cfrac{\lambda t}{1-\cfrac{t}{1-\cfrac{(\lambda+1)t}{1-\cfrac{2t}{1-\cdots}}}}}

(3.8)

with coefficients
\alpha_{2k-1}=\lambda+k-1
and
\alpha_{2k}=k
.
Now, any S-fraction has an associated production matrix

P\;=\;\begin{bmatrix}\alpha_{1}&1&&&\\
\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2}&\alpha_{2}+\alpha_{3}&1&&\\
&\alpha_{3}\alpha_{4}&\alpha_{4}+\alpha_{5}&1&\\
&&\ddots&\ddots&\ddots\end{bmatrix}

(3.9)

(see eq. (
A.7
) in Appendix
A.1
),
which has as its output matrix the triangular array of
generalized Stieltjes–Rogers polynomials of the first kind.
And the production matrix corresponding to the
coefficients
\alpha_{2k-1}=\lambda+k-1
and
\alpha_{2k}=k

is precisely (
3.1
).
In view of Proposition
1.2
(a), this shows:

Proposition 3.1
.

The Laguerre coefficient matrix
{\sf L}^{(\alpha)}

is the matrix
{\sf S}=(S_{n,\ell}({\bm{\alpha}}))_{n,\ell\geq 0}

of generalized Stieltjes–Rogers polynomials of the first kind
corresponding to the coefficients

\alpha_{2k-1}=k+\alpha
and
\alpha_{2k}=k
.

Remark.

Proposition
3.1
looks at first sight a bit bizarre,
because the coefficient matrix for the monic orthogonal polynomials
associated to a Stieltjes moment sequence
{\bm{a}}=(a_{n})_{n\geq 0}

is in general the
inverse matrix
of the corresponding matrix
{\sf S}

of generalized Stieltjes--Rogers polynomials
14
14

14

This is a well-known fact: see, for instance

[
79
, p. III-2, Théorème 1]
.
And for a generalization to unit-lower-Hessenberg production matrices
that are not necessarily tridiagonal,
see
[
68
, Proposition 3.2 and Remark 1 following it]
.

;
therefore, the coefficient matrix for the monic unsigned Laguerre polynomials
should be the
unsigned inverse matrix
of
{\sf S}
, i.e.

{\sf S}^{\sharp}\;\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\rm def}}{{=}}\;Q\,{\sf S}^{-1}\,Q

(3.10)

where
Q=((-1)^{i}\delta_{ij})_{i,j\geq 0}

is the diagonal matrix of alternating 1’s and
-1
’s.
This apparent paradox is explained by the curious fact that the
matrix
{\sf L}^{(\alpha)}
equals its own unsigned inverse:
that is,

{\sf L}^{(\alpha)}\;=\;Q\,({\sf L}^{(\alpha)})^{-1}\,Q\;.

(3.11)

Proof.
From (
1.12
) we see that

{\sf L}^{(\alpha)}=D\,B^{(\alpha)}\,D^{-1}

where
B^{(\alpha)}
is the generalized binomial matrix

(B^{(\alpha)})_{nk}\;=\;\binom{n+\alpha}{n-k}

(3.12)

and
D=\mathop{\rm diag}\nolimits\big((n!)_{n\geq 0}\big)
.
So it suffices to prove that
B^{(\alpha)}
equals its own unsigned inverse:

\sum_{j}\binom{n+\alpha}{n-j}\,\binom{j+\alpha}{j-k}\,(-1)^{j-k}&=\binom{n+\alpha}{n-k}\,\sum_{j}\binom{n-k}{j-k}\,(-1)^{j-k}\\
=\delta_{nk}

(3.13)

(cf.
[
39
, eq. (5.21)]
).

\blacksquare

3.2
Binomial row-generating matrix
{\sf L}^{\bm{(\alpha)}}\bm{B_{x}}
: Proof of Proposition
1.2
(b)

We now prove Proposition
1.2
(b),
which asserts that the production matrix
for the binomial row-generating matrix
{\sf L}^{(\alpha)}B_{x}

is the quadridiagonal unit-lower-Hessenberg matrix

P=(p_{ij})_{i,j\geq 0}
defined by

p_{n,n+1}&=1\\
p_{n,n}=(2n+1+\alpha)\,+\,x\\
p_{n,n-1}=n(n+\alpha)\,+\,2nx\\
p_{n,n-2}=n(n-1)x\\
p_{n,k}=0\qquad\textrm{if $k<n-2$ or $k>n+1$}

(3.14)

This is an easy consequence of the theory of exponential Riordan arrays.
In the preceding subsection we saw that the production matrix
P^{\circ}

for the Laguerre coefficient matrix
{\sf L}^{(\alpha)}

is the exponential AZ matrix
\mathop{\rm EAZ}\nolimits(A,Z)

where
A
and
Z
are given by (
3.5
)/(
3.7
).
By Lemma
2.7

the production matrix for
{\sf L}^{(\alpha)}B_{x}
is
P=B_{x}^{-1}P^{\circ}B_{x}
;
and by Lemma
2.22
we have

B_{x}^{-1}P^{\circ}B_{x}=\mathop{\rm EAZ}\nolimits(A,Z+xA)
.
Using this together with (
3.5
)/(
3.7
)
and (
2.37
)
yields (
3.14
).

In Appendix
B
we will explain why
P=B_{x}^{-1}P^{\circ}B_{x}

is quadridiagonal, by answering the more general question:
Which lower-Hessenberg matrices
P
have the property that

B_{\xi}^{-1}PB_{\xi}
is
(r,1)
-banded?

We also have an explicit formula for the binomial row-generating matrix:

Proposition 3.2

(Binomial row-generating matrix of the Laguerre coefficient array)
.

The matrix elements of the binomial row-generating matrix

{\sf L}^{(\alpha)}B_{x}
are

({\sf L}^{(\alpha)}B_{x})_{n,k}\;=\;{1\over k!}\,{d^{k}\over dx^{k}}\,{{\mathcal{L}}_{n}^{(\alpha)}}(x)\;=\;\binom{n}{k}\,{\mathcal{L}}_{n-k}^{(\alpha+k)}(x)\;.

(3.15)

Proof.
The first equality is a special case of the general property (
2.26
)
of binomial row-generating polynomials.
The second equality follows by repeated use of the differentiation formula

{d\over dx}{\mathcal{L}}_{n}^{(\alpha)}(x)\;=\;n{\mathcal{L}}_{n-1}^{(\alpha+1)}(x)\;.

(3.16)

\square

It can also be shown, by direct computation using classical identities
for the Laguerre polynomials,
that the matrix (
3.15
)
is indeed the output matrix for the production matrix
(
3.14
).
But since this proof is rather lengthy, we refrain from showing it here.

3.3
Total positivity of the coefficient matrix
\bm{{\sf L}^{(-1+\lambda)}}

Finally, let us give a simple direct proof of the
coefficientwise total positivity of the Laguerre
coefficient matrix
{\sf L}^{(-1+\lambda)}
.
Start from the binomial matrix
a_{nk}=\binom{n}{k}
,
which is totally positive by Lemma
2.2
.
Now use Lemma
2.3
(b) with

x_{i}=\lambda+i-1
for
i\geq 1
,
which are nonnegative elements of
{\mathbb{Z}}[\lambda]
.
Then

b_{nk}&=x_{k+1}x_{k+2}\,\cdots\,x_{n}\,a_{nk}\\
=(\lambda+k)^{\overline{n-k}}\,\binom{n}{k}\;,

(3.17)

which is (
1.12
) with
\alpha=-1+\lambda
.

4
Multivariate Laguerre polynomials:

Exponential generating functions and production matrices

In this section we compute the
column and bivariate exponential generating functions
for the multivariate Laguerre coefficient matrix (
1.35
);
we then use these exponential generating functions,
together with the theory of exponential Riordan arrays,
to deduce the corresponding production matrices.

4.1
Exponential generating functions

In this subsection we shall compute the
column and bivariate exponential generating functions
for the coefficient matrix

{\widehat{{\sf L}}}^{(\alpha)}({y_{\rm p}},{y_{\rm v}},{y_{\rm da}},{y_{\rm dd}},{y_{\rm fp}})

that was defined in (
1.35
),
which enumerates Laguerre digraphs according to the status of vertices
as peaks, valleys, double ascents, double descents or fixed points.
Indeed, we shall go farther, by assigning different weights
for the vertices belonging to a cycle or to a path.
For a Laguerre digraph
G
,
let us write
{\rm pcyc}(G)
,
{\rm vcyc}(G)
,
{\rm dacyc}(G)
,
{\rm ddcyc}(G)
,
{\rm fp}(G)

for the number of peaks, valleys, double ascents, double descents
and fixed points that belong to a cycle of
G
,
and
{\rm ppa}(G)
,
{\rm vpa}(G)
,
{\rm dapa}(G)
,
{\rm ddpa}(G)

for the number of peaks, valleys, double ascents and double descents
that belong to a path of
G

(of course fixed points can only belong to a cycle).
We then assign weights
{y_{\rm p}},{y_{\rm v}},{y_{\rm da}},{y_{\rm dd}},{y_{\rm fp}}

to the vertices belonging to a cycle,
and weights
{z_{\rm p}},{z_{\rm v}},{z_{\rm da}},{z_{\rm dd}}

to the vertices belonging to a path.
We therefore define the

generalized second multivariate Laguerre coefficient matrix

\displaystyle\!\!\!\!{\widetilde{{\sf L}}}^{(\alpha)}({y_{\rm p}},{y_{\rm v}},{y_{\rm da}},{y_{\rm dd}},{y_{\rm fp}},{z_{\rm p}},{z_{\rm v}},{z_{\rm da}},{z_{\rm dd}})_{n,k}

\displaystyle\quad\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\rm def}}{{=}}\sum_{G\in{\mathbf{LD}}_{n,k}}y_{\rm p}^{{\rm pcyc}(G)}y_{\rm v}^{{\rm vcyc}(G)}y_{\rm da}^{{\rm dacyc}(G)}y_{\rm dd}^{{\rm ddcyc}(G)}y_{\rm fp}^{{\rm fp}(G)}\,z_{\rm p}^{{\rm ppa}(G)}z_{\rm v}^{{\rm vpa}(G)}z_{\rm da}^{{\rm dapa}(G)}z_{\rm dd}^{{\rm ddpa}(G)}\>\times

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\quad\;\,\,(1+\alpha)^{{\rm cyc}(G)}\>.\qquad

(4.1)

This polynomial is homogeneous of degree
n
in

{y_{\rm p}},{y_{\rm v}},{y_{\rm da}},{y_{\rm dd}},{y_{\rm fp}},{z_{\rm p}},{z_{\rm v}},{z_{\rm da}},{z_{\rm dd}}
.

Because of the 0–0 boundary conditions,
each path contains at least one peak;
so we can, if we wish, remove a factor
z_{\rm p}^{k}

and define a unit-lower-triangular matrix by

{\widetilde{{\sf L}}}^{(\alpha)\flat}({y_{\rm p}},{y_{\rm v}},{y_{\rm da}},{y_{\rm dd}},{y_{\rm fp}},{z_{\rm p}},{z_{\rm v}},{z_{\rm da}},{z_{\rm dd}})_{n,k}\;\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\rm def}}{{=}}\;{\widetilde{{\sf L}}}^{(\alpha)}({y_{\rm p}},{y_{\rm v}},{y_{\rm da}},{y_{\rm dd}},{y_{\rm fp}},{z_{\rm p}},{z_{\rm v}},{z_{\rm da}},{z_{\rm dd}})_{n,k}/z_{\rm p}^{k}\;.

(4.2)

This polynomial is homogeneous of degree
n-k
in

{y_{\rm p}},{y_{\rm v}},{y_{\rm da}},{y_{\rm dd}},{y_{\rm fp}},{z_{\rm p}},{z_{\rm v}},{z_{\rm da}},{z_{\rm dd}}
.

We now proceed to compute the exponential generating functions
for the matrices (
4.1
)
and (
4.2
).
We do this by combining the known exponential generating functions
for permutations with cyclic statistics
[
84
, Théorème 1]

and permutations with linear statistics
[
84
, Proposition 4]
.
These formulae are as follows:

1) Permutations with cyclic statistics are enumerated by the polynomials

P_{n}^{\rm cyc}({y_{\rm p}},{y_{\rm v}},{y_{\rm da}},{y_{\rm dd}},{y_{\rm fp}},\lambda)\;\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\rm def}}{{=}}\;\sum_{\sigma\in{\mathfrak{S}}_{n}}y_{\rm p}^{{\rm pcyc}(\sigma)}y_{\rm v}^{{\rm vcyc}(\sigma)}y_{\rm da}^{{\rm dacyc}(\sigma)}y_{\rm dd}^{{\rm ddcyc}(\sigma)}y_{\rm fp}^{{\rm fp}(\sigma)}\,\lambda^{{\rm cyc}(\sigma)}

(4.3)

where
{\rm pcyc}(\sigma)
,
{\rm vcyc}(\sigma)
,
{\rm dacyc}(\sigma)
,
{\rm ddcyc}(\sigma)
,

{\rm fp}(\sigma)

denote the number of cycle peaks, cycle valleys, cycle double rises,
cycle double falls and fixed points in
\sigma
,
and
{\rm cyc}(\sigma)
denotes the number of cycles in
\sigma
.
By convention we set
P_{0}^{\rm cyc}=1
.
The polynomial
P_{n}^{\rm cyc}
is homogeneous of degree
n
in

{y_{\rm p}},{y_{\rm v}},{y_{\rm da}},{y_{\rm dd}},{y_{\rm fp}}
.
We write

F(t;{y_{\rm p}},{y_{\rm v}},{y_{\rm da}},{y_{\rm dd}},{y_{\rm fp}},\lambda)\;\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\rm def}}{{=}}\;\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}P_{n}^{\rm cyc}({y_{\rm p}},{y_{\rm v}},{y_{\rm da}},{y_{\rm dd}},{y_{\rm fp}},\lambda)\>{t^{n}\over n!}

(4.4)

for the corresponding exponential generating function.

Lemma 4.1
.

[
84
, Théorème 1]

We have

F(t;{y_{\rm p}},{y_{\rm v}},{y_{\rm da}},{y_{\rm dd}},{y_{\rm fp}},\lambda)&=e^{\lambda{y_{\rm fp}}t}\,\biggl({r_{1}\,-\,r_{2}\over r_{1}e^{r_{2}t}\,-\,r_{2}e^{r_{1}t}}\biggr)^{\!\lambda}\\
=F(t;{y_{\rm p}},{y_{\rm v}},{y_{\rm da}},{y_{\rm dd}},{y_{\rm fp}},1)^{\lambda}

(4.5)

where
r_{1}r_{2}={y_{\rm p}}{y_{\rm v}}
and
r_{1}+r_{2}={y_{\rm da}}+{y_{\rm dd}}
.
Otherwise put,
r_{1}
and
r_{2}
are the roots (in either order)
of the quadratic equation
\rho^{2}-({y_{\rm da}}+{y_{\rm dd}})\rho+{y_{\rm p}}{y_{\rm v}}=0
.
Concretely,

r_{1,2}\;=\;{{y_{\rm da}}+{y_{\rm dd}}\pm\sqrt{({y_{\rm da}}+{y_{\rm dd}})^{2}-4{y_{\rm p}}{y_{\rm v}}}\over 2}\;.

(4.6)

2) Permutations with linear statistics and 0–0 boundary conditions
are enumerated by the polynomials

P_{n}^{\rm lin(00)}({z_{\rm p}},{z_{\rm v}},{z_{\rm da}},{z_{\rm dd}})\;\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\rm def}}{{=}}\;\sum_{\sigma\in{\mathfrak{S}}_{n}}z_{\rm p}^{{\rm ppa}(\sigma)}z_{\rm v}^{{\rm vpa}(\sigma)}z_{\rm da}^{{\rm dapa}(\sigma)}z_{\rm dd}^{{\rm ddpa}(\sigma)}

(4.7)

where
{\rm ppa}(\sigma)
,
{\rm vpa}(\sigma)
,
{\rm dapa}(\sigma)
,
{\rm ddpa}(\sigma)

denote the number of peaks, valleys, double ascents and double descents
in the permutation
\sigma
written as a word
\sigma_{1}\cdots\sigma_{n}
,
where we impose the boundary conditions
\sigma_{0}=\sigma_{n+1}=0
.
By convention we restrict attention to
n\geq 1
.
The polynomial
P_{n}^{\rm lin(00)}
is homogeneous of degree
n
in

{z_{\rm p}},{z_{\rm v}},{z_{\rm da}},{z_{\rm dd}}
.
We write

G(t;{z_{\rm p}},{z_{\rm v}},{z_{\rm da}},{z_{\rm dd}})\;\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\rm def}}{{=}}\;\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}P_{n}^{\rm lin(00)}({z_{\rm p}},{z_{\rm v}},{z_{\rm da}},{z_{\rm dd}})\>{t^{n}\over n!}

(4.8)

for the corresponding exponential generating function
(note that the sum starts at
n=1
).

Lemma 4.2
.

[
84
, Proposition 4]

We have

G(t;{z_{\rm p}},{z_{\rm v}},{z_{\rm da}},{z_{\rm dd}})\;=\;{z_{\rm p}}\>\biggl({e^{r_{1}t}\,-\,e^{r_{2}t}\over r_{1}e^{r_{2}t}\,-\,r_{2}e^{r_{1}t}}\biggr)

(4.9)

where
r_{1}r_{2}={z_{\rm p}}{z_{\rm v}}
and
r_{1}+r_{2}={z_{\rm da}}+{z_{\rm dd}}

analogously to Lemma
4.1
.
This function satisfies the differential equation

G^{\prime}(t)\;=\;{z_{\rm p}}\,+\,({z_{\rm da}}+{z_{\rm dd}})G(t)\,+\,{z_{\rm v}}G(t)^{2}\;.

(4.10)

We can now put these ingredients together to determine
the exponential generating functions
for the matrix (
4.1
).
A Laguerre digraph
G\in{\mathbf{LD}}_{n,k}

consists of a permutation (that is, a collection of disjoint cycles)
on some subset
S\subseteq[n]

together with
k
disjoint paths on
[n]\setminus S
.
Each of these paths can be considered as a permutation written in word form.
By the exponential formula, the exponential generating function
for the
k
th column of the matrix (
4.1
) is then

\displaystyle\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}{\widetilde{{\sf L}}}^{(\alpha)}({y_{\rm p}},{y_{\rm v}},{y_{\rm da}},{y_{\rm dd}},{y_{\rm fp}},{z_{\rm p}},{z_{\rm v}},{z_{\rm da}},{z_{\rm dd}})_{n,k}\>{t^{n}\over n!}

\displaystyle\qquad=\;F(t;{y_{\rm p}},{y_{\rm v}},{y_{\rm da}},{y_{\rm dd}},{y_{\rm fp}},1)^{1+\alpha}\;{G(t;{z_{\rm p}},{z_{\rm v}},{z_{\rm da}},{z_{\rm dd}})^{k}\over k!}\;,\qquad\qquad

(4.11)

where the
1/k!
comes because the paths are indistinguishable.
The bivariate exponential generating function is therefore

\displaystyle\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\sum_{k=0}^{n}{\widetilde{{\sf L}}}^{(\alpha)}({y_{\rm p}},{y_{\rm v}},{y_{\rm da}},{y_{\rm dd}},{y_{\rm fp}},{z_{\rm p}},{z_{\rm v}},{z_{\rm da}},{z_{\rm dd}})_{n,k}\>{t^{n}\over n!}\,u^{k}

\displaystyle\qquad=\;F(t;{y_{\rm p}},{y_{\rm v}},{y_{\rm da}},{y_{\rm dd}},{y_{\rm fp}},1)^{1+\alpha}\;\exp\bigl[u\,G(t;{z_{\rm p}},{z_{\rm v}},{z_{\rm da}},{z_{\rm dd}})\bigr]\;.\qquad\qquad

(4.12)

Comparing this with (
2.32
)/(
2.33
)
shows:

Proposition 4.3

(Generalized second multivariate Laguerre coefficient matrix as exponential Riordan array)
.

The matrix

{\widetilde{{\sf L}}}^{(\alpha)}({y_{\rm p}},{y_{\rm v}},{y_{\rm da}},{y_{\rm dd}},{y_{\rm fp}},{z_{\rm p}},{z_{\rm v}},{z_{\rm da}},{z_{\rm dd}})
,
which was defined in (
4.1
),
is an exponential Riordan array
{\mathcal{R}}[F,G]

where
F
is given by (
4.5
) with
\lambda=1+\alpha

and
G
is given by (
4.9
).

For the matrix
{\widetilde{{\sf L}}}^{(\alpha)\flat}

defined in (
4.2
),
the formulae are the same except that
G
is replaced by

G^{\flat}(t;{z_{\rm p}},{z_{\rm v}},{z_{\rm da}},{z_{\rm dd}})\;\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\rm def}}{{=}}\;G(t;{z_{\rm p}},{z_{\rm v}},{z_{\rm da}},{z_{\rm dd}})/{z_{\rm p}}\;=\;{e^{r_{1}t}\,-\,e^{r_{2}t}\over r_{1}e^{r_{2}t}\,-\,r_{2}e^{r_{1}t}}\;,

(4.13)

which satisfies the differential equation

(G^{\flat})^{\prime}(t)\;=\;1\,+\,({z_{\rm da}}+{z_{\rm dd}})G^{\flat}(t)\,+\,{z_{\rm p}}{z_{\rm v}}G^{\flat}(t)^{2}\;.

(4.14)

Remark.

For the first multivariate coefficient matrix (
1.23
),
the formulae simplify:
taking
{y_{\rm p}}={y_{\rm dd}}={z_{\rm p}}={z_{\rm dd}}=v_{-}
,

{y_{\rm v}}={y_{\rm da}}={z_{\rm v}}={z_{\rm da}}=v_{-}
and
{y_{\rm fp}}=v_{0}
,
we get
r_{1}=v_{+}
,
r_{2}=v_{-}
and hence,
using (
1.37
),

\displaystyle\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\sum_{k=0}^{n}{\sf L}^{(\alpha)}(v_{-},v_{0},v_{+})_{n,k}\>{t^{n}\over n!}\,u^{k}

\displaystyle\qquad=\;e^{(1+\alpha)v_{0}t}\>\biggl({v_{+}\,-\,v_{-}\over v_{+}e^{v_{-}t}\,-\,v_{-}e^{v_{+}t}}\biggr)^{\!1+\alpha}\>\exp\Biggl[u\,\biggl({e^{v_{+}t}\,-\,e^{v_{-}t}\over v_{+}e^{v_{-}t}\,-\,v_{-}e^{v_{+}t}}\biggr)\Biggr]\;.\qquad\qquad

(4.15)

4.2
Production matrices: Proof of Proposition
1.5
(a,b)

We now restrict attention to the case in which the vertices belonging
to paths and cycles are given the same weights,
i.e.
({z_{\rm p}},{z_{\rm v}},{z_{\rm da}},{z_{\rm dd}})=({y_{\rm p}},{y_{\rm v}},{y_{\rm da}},{y_{\rm dd}})
.
In this situation it is easy to find the series
A(s)
and
Z(s)

that satisfy the relations (
2.39
)
leading to the production matrix of an exponential Riordan array.
Namely, from (
4.10
) we have immediately

A(s)\;=\;{y_{\rm p}}\,+\,({y_{\rm da}}+{y_{\rm dd}})s\,+\,{y_{\rm v}}s^{2}\;.

(4.16)

And a straightforward computation shows that
the logarithmic derivative of
F
equals
\lambda{y_{\rm fp}}+\lambda{y_{\rm v}}G
,
so we have

Z(s)\;=\;\lambda{y_{\rm fp}}+\lambda{y_{\rm v}}s\;.

(4.17)

Theorem
2.21
then yields:

Proposition 4.4

(Production matrix of the second multivariate Laguerre coefficient matrix)
.

The second multivariate Laguerre coefficient matrix

{\widehat{{\sf L}}}^{(\alpha)}({y_{\rm p}},{y_{\rm v}},{y_{\rm da}},{y_{\rm dd}},{y_{\rm fp}})
,
which was defined in (
1.35
),
has production matrix
P^{\circ}=\mathop{\rm EAZ}\nolimits(A,Z)
where
A
and
Z
are given by
(
4.16
)/(
4.17
)
with
\lambda=1+\alpha
.
In detail,
P^{\circ}
is the tridiagonal matrix

p^{\circ}_{n,n+1}&={y_{\rm p}}\\
p^{\circ}_{n,n}=(1+\alpha){y_{\rm fp}}\,+\,n({y_{\rm da}}+{y_{\rm dd}})\\
p^{\circ}_{n,n-1}=n(n+\alpha){y_{\rm v}}\\
p^{\circ}_{n,k}=0\qquad\textrm{if $k<n-1$ or $k>n+1$}

(4.18)

From this it is straightforward to deduce the production matrix
for the binomial row-generating matrix

{\widehat{{\sf L}}}^{(\alpha)}({y_{\rm p}},{y_{\rm v}},{y_{\rm da}},{y_{\rm dd}},{y_{\rm fp}})\,B_{x}
.
Namely, by Lemma
2.7

this production matrix is
P=B_{x}^{-1}P^{\circ}B_{x}
;
and by Lemma
2.22
we have

B_{x}^{-1}P^{\circ}B_{x}=\mathop{\rm EAZ}\nolimits(A,Z+xA)
.
We have therefore proven:

Proposition 4.5

(Production matrix of the second multivariate Laguerre binomial row-generating matrix)
.

The binomial row-generating matrix

{\widehat{{\sf L}}}^{(\alpha)}({y_{\rm p}},{y_{\rm v}},{y_{\rm da}},{y_{\rm dd}},{y_{\rm fp}})\,B_{x}

has production matrix
P=\mathop{\rm EAZ}\nolimits(A,Z+xA)
where
A
and
Z
are given by
(
4.16
)/(
4.17
)
with
\lambda=1+\alpha
.
In detail,
P
is the quadridiagonal lower-Hessenberg matrix

p_{n,n+1}&={y_{\rm p}}\\
p_{n,n}=(1+\alpha){y_{\rm fp}}\,+\,n({y_{\rm da}}+{y_{\rm dd}})\,+\,{y_{\rm p}}x\\
p_{n,n-1}=n(n+\alpha){y_{\rm v}}\,+\,n({y_{\rm da}}+{y_{\rm dd}})x\\
p_{n,n-2}=n(n-1){y_{\rm v}}x\\
p_{n,k}=0\qquad\textrm{if $k<n-2$ or $k>n+1$}

(4.19)

For the matrix
{\widetilde{{\sf L}}}^{(\alpha)\flat}
,
the formulae are the same except that
A
and
Z
are replaced by

\displaystyle A^{\flat}(s)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 1\,+\,({y_{\rm da}}+{y_{\rm dd}})s\,+\,{y_{\rm p}}{y_{\rm v}}s^{2}

(4.20)

\displaystyle Z^{\flat}(s)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\lambda{y_{\rm fp}}+\lambda{y_{\rm p}}{y_{\rm v}}s

(4.21)

This leads to the production matrices

p^{\circ\flat}_{n,n+1}&=1\\
p^{\circ\flat}_{n,n}=(1+\alpha){y_{\rm fp}}\,+\,n({y_{\rm da}}+{y_{\rm dd}})\\
p^{\circ\flat}_{n,n-1}=n(n+\alpha){y_{\rm p}}{y_{\rm v}}\\
p^{\circ\flat}_{n,k}=0\qquad\textrm{if $k<n-1$ or $k>n+1$}

(4.22)

and

p^{\flat}_{n,n+1}&=1\\
p^{\flat}_{n,n}=(1+\alpha){y_{\rm fp}}\,+\,n({y_{\rm da}}+{y_{\rm dd}})\,+\,x\\
p^{\flat}_{n,n-1}=n(n+\alpha){y_{\rm p}}{y_{\rm v}}\,+\,n({y_{\rm da}}+{y_{\rm dd}})x\\
p^{\flat}_{n,n-2}=n(n-1){y_{\rm p}}{y_{\rm v}}x\\
p^{\flat}_{n,k}=0\qquad\textrm{if $k<n-2$ or $k>n+1$}

(4.23)

This completes the proof of Proposition
1.5
.

5
Total positivity of the production matrices:

Univariate case

In this section we will prove the total positivity
of the production matrices for the univariate Laguerre polynomials:
namely, the tridiagonal production matrix
P^{\circ}

[defined in (
1.44
)]
for the Laguerre coefficient matrix
{\sf L}^{(\alpha)}
,
and the quadridiagonal production matrix
P

[defined in (
1.45
)]
for the binomial row-generating matrix
{\sf L}^{(\alpha)}\,B_{x}
.
Throughout, we use the parameter
\lambda=1+\alpha
.

The proofs are easy factorizations;
in the quadridiagonal case we will also need to invoke the
tridiagonal comparison theorem (Proposition
2.6
).

5.1
The tridiagonal matrix
\bm{P^{\circ}}
: Proof of Proposition
1.3
(a)

The tridiagonal matrix
P^{\circ}
is given by

p^{\circ}_{n,n+1}&=1\\
p^{\circ}_{n,n}=2n+\lambda\\
p^{\circ}_{n,n-1}=n(n-1+\lambda)\\
p^{\circ}_{n,k}=0\qquad\textrm{if $k<n-1$ or $k>n+1$}

(5.1)

As observed preceding Proposition
3.1
,
this is the production matrix (
A.7
)
corresponding to the classical S-fraction with coefficients

\alpha_{2k-1}=k-1+\lambda
and
\alpha_{2k}=k

(see Appendix
A.1
).
And as observed in (
A.8
),
the production matrix (
A.7
)
can be factorized in the form
LU
where

L
is the lower-bidiagonal matrix with 1 on the diagonal
and
\alpha_{2},\alpha_{4},\ldots
on the subdiagonal,
and
U
is the upper-bidiagonal matrix with 1 on the superdiagonal
and
\alpha_{1},\alpha_{3},\ldots
on the diagonal.
And finally, as also observed there,
this factorization shows, by Lemma
2.1
,
that the matrix (
A.7
) is coefficientwise totally positive
in the indeterminates
{\bm{\alpha}}
.
Putting this all together, we conclude:

Proposition 5.1

(Factorization and total positivity of
P^{\circ}
)
.

(a)

The tridiagonal matrix
P^{\circ}
defined by
(
5.1
)
has the factorization
P^{\circ}=LU
where

\bullet

L
is the lower-bidiagonal matrix with 1 on the diagonal
and
1,2,3,\ldots
on the subdiagonal, and

\bullet

U
is the upper-bidiagonal matrix with 1 on the
superdiagonal and
\lambda,{\lambda+1},

{\lambda+2},\ldots
on the diagonal.

(b)

P^{\circ}
is totally positive in the ring
{\mathbb{Z}}[\lambda]

equipped with the coefficientwise order.

In particular, this proves Proposition
1.3
(a).

Note that we can also write
U=L\Delta+\lambda I
,
since
L\Delta
is the upper-bidiagonal matrix with 1 on the superdiagonal
and
0,1,2,\ldots
on the diagonal.

5.2
The quadridiagonal matrix
\bm{P}
: Proof of Proposition
1.3
(b)

The quadridiagonal matrix
P
is given by

p_{n,n+1}&=1\\
p_{n,n}=(2n+\lambda)\,+\,x\\
p_{n,n-1}=n(n-1+\lambda)\,+\,2nx\\
p_{n,n-2}=n(n-1)x\\
p_{n,k}=0\qquad\textrm{if $k<n-2$ or $k>n+1$}

(5.2)

Proposition 5.2

(Factorization and total positivity of
P
)
.

(a)

The quadridiagonal matrix
P
defined by
(
5.2
)
has the factorization
P={L(LU_{x}+\lambda I)}
where

\bullet

L
is the lower-bidiagonal matrix with 1 on the diagonal
and
1,2,3,\ldots
on the subdiagonal, and

\bullet

U_{x}=\Delta+xI
is the upper-bidiagonal matrix
with 1 on the superdiagonal and
x
on the diagonal.

(b)

P
is totally positive in the ring
{\mathbb{Z}}[x,\lambda]

equipped with the coefficientwise order.

Proof.
(a) It is easy to see that
P=P^{\circ}+xLL
.
Since
P^{\circ}=L(L\Delta+\lambda I)

by Proposition
5.1
(a),
we have
P=L[L(\Delta+xI)+\lambda I]
,
which proves part (a).

(b) By Lemma
2.1
, the bidiagonal matrices
L
and
U_{x}

are totally positive in the ring
{\mathbb{Z}}[x]

equipped with the coefficientwise order.
It follows that the tridiagonal matrix
LU_{x}

is also totally positive in the ring
{\mathbb{Z}}[x]

equipped with the coefficientwise order.
Then the tridiagonal comparison theorem (Proposition
2.6
)
shows that the tridiagonal matrix
LU_{x}+\lambda I

is totally positive in the ring
{\mathbb{Z}}[x,\lambda]

equipped with the coefficientwise order.
Therefore the same holds for
P=L(LU_{x}+\lambda I)
.

\square

This completes the proof of Proposition
1.3
(b).

Remarks.

1. For
\lambda=0
(
\alpha=-1
), we have
P=LLU_{x}
,
which is the production matrix for the known 2-S-fraction
for the Lah polynomials
[
58
, Theorem 1.5 with
r=2
]
,
which has coefficients

\alpha_{3n-1}&=x\\
\alpha_{3n}=n\\
\alpha_{3n+1}=n

(5.3)

(For the production matrix associated to a 2-S-fraction,
see
[
59
, Propositions 7.2 and 8.2 and eqn. (7.8)]

or the case
j=0
of Proposition
A.3
below.)
However, it turns out that this same production matrix
has a one-parameter family of distinct factorizations
P=L_{1}L_{2}U_{x}
,
arising from the 2-S-fractions with

\alpha_{3n-1}&=x\\
\alpha_{3n}=c_{n}\,n\\
\alpha_{3n+1}=(2-c_{n})\,n

(5.4)

where

c_{n}\;\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\rm def}}{{=}}\;{(n-1)\,-\,(n-2)\kappa\over n\,-\,(n-1)\kappa}\quad\hbox{for }n\geq 1

(5.5)

and
\kappa\in[0,1]
:
see Proposition
A.8
below.
The case
\kappa=1
is the known 2-S-fraction;
all the others appear to be new.

2. It is easy to see that
L\Delta+I=\Delta L

(this is a special case of (
A.23
) below)
and hence
LU_{x}+I=U_{x}L
.
This implies that for
\lambda=1
(
\alpha=0
), we have

P=L(LU_{x}+I)=LU_{x}L
.
This is the production matrix for the
generalized 2-Stieltjes–Rogers polynomials of type
j=1

with the coefficients (
5.3
):
see Proposition
A.3
for the general theory,
and Proposition
A.12
for this application.

\blacksquare

6
Total positivity of the production matrices:

Multivariate case

In this section we will prove
Proposition
1.6

on the total positivity of the production matrices
for the multivariate Laguerre polynomials.
The proof of Proposition
1.6
(a),
dealing with the tridiagonal matrix
P^{\circ\flat}
,
is an easy factorization
combined with an appeal to the tridiagonal comparison theorem.
By contrast,
the proof of Proposition
1.6
(b),
dealing with the quadridiagonal matrix
P^{\flat}
,
is decidedly nontrivial.

6.1
The tridiagonal matrix
\bm{P^{\circ\flat}}
: Proof of Proposition
1.6
(a)

Proof of Proposition
1.6
(a)
.

Let
Q
the matrix (
A.8
)
with
\alpha_{2k-1}=(k+\alpha){y_{\rm p}}
and
\alpha_{2k}=k{y_{\rm v}}
for
k\geq 1
;
note that these coefficients are nonnegative in the ring
R

because of the hypotheses
\alpha\geq-1
,
{y_{\rm p}}\geq 0
and
{y_{\rm v}}\geq 0
.
It then follows from Lemma
2.1

that
Q
is totally positive in the ring
R
.
The matrix
Q
has elements

q_{n,n+1}&=1\\
q_{n,n}=(1+\alpha){y_{\rm p}}\,+\,n({y_{\rm p}}+{y_{\rm v}})\\
q_{n,n-1}=n(n+\alpha){y_{\rm p}}{y_{\rm v}}\\
q_{n,k}=0\qquad\textrm{if $k<n-1$ or $k>n+1$}

(6.1)

Then
P^{\circ\flat}=Q+D
,
where
D
is the diagonal matrix with entries

d_{n}={(1+\alpha)({y_{\rm fp}}-{y_{\rm p}})}+{n({y_{\rm da}}+{y_{\rm dd}}-{y_{\rm p}}-{y_{\rm v}})}
.
By hypothesis these entries are nonnegative in the ring
R
,
so the tridiagonal comparison theorem (Proposition
2.6
)
implies that
P^{\circ\flat}
is totally positive in the ring
R
.

\square

6.2
The quadridiagonal matrix
\bm{P^{\flat}}
: Proof of Proposition
1.6
(b)

We will now prove Proposition
1.6
(b),
which asserts the total positivity
of the production matrix
(
1.47
)
when the variables are substituted to elements
of a partially ordered commutative ring
R

that satisfy
\lambda\geq 0
,

\lambda{y_{\rm fp}}=\lambda{y_{\rm p}}
,
{y_{\rm p}}\geq 0
,
{y_{\rm v}}\geq 0
,

{y_{\rm da}}+{y_{\rm dd}}\geq{y_{\rm p}}+{y_{\rm v}}
and
x\geq 0
.
We will do this by proving the
coefficientwise
total positivity
of a
much more general
quadridiagonal lower-Hessenberg matrix
P
,
defined by

P&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\rm def}}{{=}}L_{1}UL_{2}\,+\,L_{1}D_{1}\,+\,D_{2}L_{2}{}\\
=L_{1}(UL_{2}\,+\,D_{1})\,+\,D_{2}L_{2}{}\\
=(L_{1}U\,+\,D_{2})L_{2}\,+\,L_{1}D_{1}{}

(6.2)

where

•

L_{1}
is the lower-bidiagonal matrix with
the sequence
a_{0},a_{1},\ldots
on the diagonal,
the sequence
b_{1},b_{2},\ldots
on the subdiagonal,
and zeroes elsewhere;

•

U
is the upper-bidiagonal matrix with
the sequence
c_{1},c_{2},\ldots
on the superdiagonal,
the sequence
d_{0},d_{1},\ldots
on the diagonal,
and zeroes elsewhere;

•

L_{2}
is the lower-bidiagonal matrix with
the sequence
e_{0},e_{1},\ldots
on the diagonal,
the sequence
f_{1},f_{2},\ldots
on the subdiagonal,
and zeroes elsewhere;

•

D_{1}
is the diagonal matrix with entries
g_{0},g_{1},\ldots\,
;

•

D_{2}
is the diagonal matrix with entries
h_{0},h_{1},\ldots\,
;

and
{\mathbf{a}}=(a_{n})_{n\geq 0}
,
{\mathbf{b}}=(b_{n})_{n\geq 1}
,

{\mathbf{c}}=(c_{n})_{n\geq 1}
,
{\mathbf{d}}=(d_{n})_{n\geq 0}
,

{\mathbf{e}}=(e_{n})_{n\geq 0}
,
{\mathbf{f}}=(f_{n})_{n\geq 1}
,

{\mathbf{g}}=(g_{n})_{n\geq 0}
, and
{\mathbf{h}}=(h_{n})_{n\geq 0}

are all indeterminates.
The entries in the
n
th row of
P=(p_{n,k})_{k\geq 0}

are given by

p_{n,n+1}&=a_{n}c_{n+1}e_{n+1}\\
p_{n,n}=a_{n}d_{n}e_{n}+b_{n}c_{n}e_{n}+a_{n}c_{n+1}f_{n+1}+a_{n}g_{n}+h_{n}e_{n}\\
p_{n,n-1}=a_{n}d_{n}f_{n}+b_{n}c_{n}f_{n}+b_{n}d_{n-1}e_{n-1}+b_{n}g_{n-1}+h_{n}f_{n}\\
p_{n,n-2}=b_{n}d_{n-1}f_{n-1}\\
p_{n,k}=0\qquad\textrm{if $k<n-2$ or $k>n+1$}

(6.3)

where by definition
b_{0}=c_{0}=f_{0}=0

and
a_{n}=b_{n}=c_{n}=d_{n}=e_{n}=f_{n}=g_{n}=h_{n}=0
whenever
n<0
.
Our main result is:

Theorem 6.1

(Total positivity of the generalized production matrix)
.

The matrix
P
defined by
(
6.2
)/(
6.3
)
is totally positive, coefficientwise in the indeterminates

{\mathbf{a}},{\mathbf{b}},{\mathbf{c}},{\mathbf{d}},{\mathbf{e}},{\mathbf{f}},{\mathbf{g}},{\mathbf{h}}
.

Proof of Proposition
1.6
(b)
assuming Theorem
6.1
.

Specialize the matrix (
6.3
) by setting

&a_{n}\;=\;c_{n}\;=\;e_{n}\;=\;1\\
b_{n}\;=\;n{y_{\rm v}}\\
d_{n}\;=\;n{y_{\rm p}}\\
f_{n}\;=\;x\\
g_{n}\;=\;\lambda{y_{\rm p}}\>(=\lambda{y_{\rm fp}})\\
h_{n}\;=\;n({y_{\rm da}}+{y_{\rm dd}}-{y_{\rm p}}-{y_{\rm v}})

(6.4)

\square

Theorem
6.1
will be proven as follows.
Define the matrix
Q=(q_{n,k})_{n,k\geq 0}
by

Q\;\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\rm def}}{{=}}\;P\big|_{{\mathbf{h}}={\bm{0}}}\;=\;L_{1}(UL_{2}\,+\,D_{1})\;,

(6.5)

or in other words

q_{n,n+1}&=a_{n}c_{n+1}e_{n+1}\\
q_{n,n}=a_{n}d_{n}e_{n}+b_{n}c_{n}e_{n}+a_{n}c_{n+1}f_{n+1}+a_{n}g_{n}\\
q_{n,n-1}=a_{n}d_{n}f_{n}+b_{n}c_{n}f_{n}+b_{n}d_{n-1}e_{n-1}+b_{n}g_{n-1}\\
q_{n,n-2}=b_{n}d_{n-1}f_{n-1}\\
q_{n,k}=0\qquad\textrm{if $k<n-2$ or $k>n+1$}

(6.6)

Then

P\;=\;Q\,+\,D_{2}L_{2}.

(6.7)

We will begin by proving (Lemma
6.6
)
that
Q
is coefficientwise totally positive;
this proof uses the factorization
Q=L_{1}(UL_{2}+D_{1})

together with the tridiagonal comparison theorem,
in close analogy with Propositions
1.6
(a)
and
5.2
.
It follows that for every integer
m\geq 0
,
the matrix consisting of first
m
rows of
Q
is also
coefficientwise totally positive.

The rest of the proof shows how to restore the terms in
P
involving
{\mathbf{h}}
.
In terms of the row vectors

({\bm{p}}_{n})_{n\geq 0}
,
({\bm{q}}_{n})_{n\geq 0}
,
({\bm{\ell}}_{n})_{n\geq 0}

associated to the matrices
P,Q,L_{2}
,
equation (
6.7
) can be rewritten as

{\bm{p}}_{n}\;=\;{\bm{q}}_{n}\,+\,h_{n}{\bm{\ell}}_{n}\;,

(6.8)

where

{\bm{\ell}}_{0}&=\begin{bmatrix}e_{0}&{\bm{0}}_{1\times\infty}\end{bmatrix}\\
{\bm{\ell}}_{n}=\begin{bmatrix}{\bm{0}}_{1\times n-1}&f_{n}&e_{n}&{\bm{0}}_{1\times\infty}\end{bmatrix}\quad\textrm{for $n\geq 1$}

(6.9)

Remark.

Here we have chosen to write

Q\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\rm def}}{{=}}P|_{{\mathbf{h}}={\bm{0}}}=L_{1}(UL_{2}\,+\,D_{1})

and
P=Q+D_{2}L_{2}
and to argue by rows
(because
D_{2}
acts on the left).
We could equally well have started from

Q^{\prime}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\rm def}}{{=}}P|_{{\mathbf{g}}={\bm{0}}}=(L_{1}U\,+\,D_{2})L_{2}

and
P=Q^{\prime}+L_{1}D_{1}
and to argue by columns
(because
D_{1}
acts on the right).
The reader is invited to work out this variant proof.

\blacksquare

Our proof is based on considering the matrix

\displaystyle\begin{bmatrix}{\bm{q}}_{0}\\
\vdots\\
{\bm{q}}_{n-1}\\
{\bm{p}}_{n}\\
\vdots\\
{\bm{p}}_{m}\end{bmatrix}
,
which we shall sometimes write henceforth for typographical simplicity
as
[[{\bm{q}}_{0},\ldots,{\bm{q}}_{n-1},{\bm{p}}_{n},\ldots,{\bm{p}}_{m}]]

(and likewise for other matrices written by rows).
We will show (Lemma
6.10
)
that for every pair of integers
0\leq n\leq m+1
,
this matrix
is totally positive;
and we will do this, for each fixed
m\geq 0
,
by induction on
n=m+1,m,m-1,\ldots,0
.
The base case
n=m+1
of this induction is thus Lemma
6.6
,
and the final case
n=0
is Theorem
6.1
.
The proof of Lemma
6.10
will involve the following steps:

Lemma
6.7
:

The matrix
[[{\bm{q}}_{0},\ldots,{\bm{q}}_{n-1},{\bm{\ell}}_{n}]]

is totally positive.

Lemma
6.8
:

If the matrix
[[{\bm{p}}_{n+1},\ldots,{\bm{p}}_{m}]]

is totally positive,

then so is
[[{\bm{q}}_{0},\ldots,{\bm{q}}_{n-1},{\bm{\ell}}_{n},{\bm{p}}_{n+1},\ldots,{\bm{p}}_{m}]]
.

The induction step (Lemma
6.9
):

If the matrix

[[{\bm{q}}_{0},\ldots,{\bm{q}}_{n},{\bm{p}}_{n+1},\ldots,{\bm{p}}_{m}]]

is totally positive,
then so is

[[{\bm{q}}_{0},\ldots,{\bm{q}}_{n-1},{\bm{p}}_{n},\ldots,{\bm{p}}_{m}]]
.

Putting this all together will prove Lemma
6.10

and hence Theorem
6.1
.

As preparation, we need some simple lemmas
concerning operations that preserve total positivity.
All of these hold in an arbitrary partially ordered commutative ring,
and apply to rectangular (i.e. not necessarily square)
matrices of suitably conformable dimensions.

Lemma 6.2

(Increasing the upper-left entry)
.

Let
A
be a (finite or infinite) matrix
with entries in a partially ordered commutative ring
R
;
let
c
be a nonnegative element of
R
;
and let
A^{\prime}
be the matrix obtained from
A

by adding
c
to the upper-left entry
and leaving all other entries unchanged.
If
A
is totally positive of order
r
, then so is
A^{\prime}
.

Proof.
Consider a minor on rows
I
and columns
J
.
If
I
and
J
do not both contain 1,
then obviously
\det(A^{\prime})_{IJ}=\det A_{IJ}
.
If
I
and
J
both contain 1,
then
\det(A^{\prime})_{IJ}=\det A_{IJ}+c\det A_{I\setminus 1,J\setminus 1}
.

\square

Lemma 6.3

(Matrices that coincide except in one row)
.

Let
\displaystyle A=\begin{bmatrix}M\\
{\bm{\alpha}}\\
N\end{bmatrix}

and
\displaystyle B=\begin{bmatrix}M\\
{\bm{\beta}}\\
N\end{bmatrix}

be matrices that coincide except in one row;
let
a,b\geq 0
;
and let

\displaystyle C=\begin{bmatrix}M\\
a{\bm{\alpha}}+b{\bm{\beta}}\\
N\end{bmatrix}
.
If
A
and
B
are totally positive of order
r
,
then so is
C
.

Proof.
This is an immediate consequence of the row linearity of determinants.

\square

Of course, an analogous result holds for matrices that coincide
except in one column.

Lemma 6.4

(Block-diagonal matrices)
.

If the matrices
A
and
B
are totally positive of order
r
,
then so is the matrix

\displaystyle C=\left[\begin{array}[]{c|c}A&\\
\hline\cr&B\end{array}\right]
.

Proof.
This is trivial: every nonzero minor of
C

is a product of minors of
A
and
B
.

\square

A slightly less trivial result
[
42
, p. 398]

concerns matrices that are not quite block-diagonal,
being shifted so as to overlap in one row:

Lemma 6.5

(Almost-block-diagonal matrices)
.

Let
A=\displaystyle\begin{bmatrix}A^{\prime}\\
{\bm{\alpha}}\end{bmatrix}

and
B=\displaystyle\begin{bmatrix}{\bm{\beta}}\\
B^{\prime}\end{bmatrix}

be two matrices,
where the row vector
{\bm{\alpha}}
is the last row of
A

and the row vector
{\bm{\beta}}
is the first row of
B
.
Now form the matrix

M\;=\;\left[\begin{array}[]{c|c}A^{\prime}&{\bm{0}}\\
\hline\cr{\bm{\alpha}}&{\bm{\beta}}\\
\hline\cr{\bm{0}}&B^{\prime}\end{array}\right]\;,

(6.10)

in which the blocks
A
and
B
overlap in one row.
If
A
and
B
are totally positive of order
r
, then so is
M
.

Proof.
By Lemma
6.4
, the block-diagonal matrices

M_{1}\;\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\rm def}}{{=}}\;\left[\begin{array}[]{c|c}\\[-8.53581pt]
A&\\[5.69054pt]
\hline\cr&B^{\prime}\end{array}\right]\;=\;\left[\begin{array}[]{c|c}A^{\prime}&\\
\hline\cr{\bm{\alpha}}&{\bm{0}}\\
\hline\cr&B^{\prime}\end{array}\right]\quad\hbox{and}\quad M_{2}\;\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\rm def}}{{=}}\;\left[\begin{array}[]{c|c}A^{\prime}&\\
\hline\cr\\[-5.69054pt]
&B\\[5.69054pt]
\end{array}\right]\;=\;\left[\begin{array}[]{c|c}A^{\prime}&\\
\hline\cr{\bm{0}}&{\bm{\beta}}\\
\hline\cr&B^{\prime}\end{array}\right]

(6.11)

are totally positive of order
r
.
But then, by Lemma
6.3
, so is
M
.

\square

Of course, an analogous result holds for matrices that overlap in one column.

We now begin the proof of Theorem
6.1
.

Lemma 6.6

(Total positivity of
Q
)
.

The matrix
Q
defined by (
6.5
)/(
6.6
)
is totally positive, coefficientwise in the indeterminates

{\mathbf{a}},{\mathbf{b}},{\mathbf{c}},{\mathbf{d}},{\mathbf{e}},{\mathbf{f}},{\mathbf{g}}
.

In particular, for every integer
m\geq 0
,
the matrix

[[{\bm{q}}_{0},\ldots,{\bm{q}}_{m}]]

is coefficientwise totally positive.

Proof.
From (
6.2
)/(
6.5
)
we have

Q\;=\;L_{1}(UL_{2}\,+\,D_{1})\;.

(6.12)

By Lemma
2.1
,
the matrices
L_{1}
,
L_{2}
,
U
and
D_{1}
are all totally positive.
Therefore,
UL_{2}
is totally positive and tridiagonal,
while
D_{1}
is totally positive and diagonal.
The tridiagonal comparison theorem (Proposition
2.6
)
then implies that
UL_{2}+D_{1}
is totally positive.
The result then follows.

\square

Lemma 6.7
.

For each integer
n\geq 0
,
the matrix

[[{\bm{q}}_{0},\ldots,{\bm{q}}_{n-1},{\bm{\ell}}_{n}]]

is coefficientwise totally positive.

Proof.
The case when
n=0
is trivial.
So we assume that
n\geq 1
.

Let
\widetilde{{\bm{q}}}_{n-1}=(\widetilde{q}_{n-1,k})_{k\geq 0}

be the row vector with entries

\widetilde{q}_{n-1,n}&=a_{n-1}c_{n}e_{n}\\
\widetilde{q}_{n-1,n-1}=a_{n-1}d_{n-1}e_{n-1}+b_{n-1}c_{n-1}e_{n-1}+a_{n-1}g_{n-1}\\
\widetilde{q}_{n-1,n-2}=a_{n-1}d_{n-1}f_{n-1}+b_{n-1}c_{n-1}f_{n-1}+b_{n-1}d_{n-2}e_{n-2}+b_{n-1}g_{n-2}\qquad\\
\widetilde{q}_{n-1,n-3}=b_{n-1}d_{n-2}f_{n-2}\\
\widetilde{q}_{n-1,k}=0\qquad\textrm{if $k<n-3$ or $k>n$}

(6.13)

Thus,
\widetilde{{\bm{q}}}_{n-1}
is identical to
{\bm{q}}_{n-1}
except
that the entry
\widetilde{q}_{n-1,n-1}
does not contain
the summand
a_{n-1}c_{n}f_{n}
.
Also, let
\widetilde{{\bm{\ell}}}_{n}=(\widetilde{l}_{n,k})_{k\geq 0}

be the row vector

\widetilde{{\bm{\ell}}}_{n}\;=\;\begin{bmatrix}{\bm{0}}_{1\times n}&e_{n}&{\bm{0}}_{1\times\infty}\end{bmatrix}\;.

(6.14)

Then

q_{n-1,n-1}&=\widetilde{q}_{n-1,n-1}\,+\,\frac{f_{n}}{e_{n}}\,\widetilde{q}_{n-1,n}\\
l_{n,n-1}=\widetilde{l}_{n,n-1}\,+\,\frac{f_{n}}{e_{n}}\,\widetilde{l}_{n,n}

(6.15)

(actually
\widetilde{l}_{n,n-1}=0
but it is convenient to write it anyway).

By Lemma
6.6
, the matrix

M_{1}\;=\;[[{\bm{q}}_{0},\ldots,{\bm{q}}_{n-2},{\bm{q}}_{n-1}]]

(6.16)

is coefficientwise totally positive.
Therefore the matrix

M_{2}\;=\;[[{\bm{q}}_{0},\ldots,{\bm{q}}_{n-2},\widetilde{{\bm{q}}}_{n-1}]]\;,

(6.17)

which is obtained from
M_{1}
by setting
f_{n}=0
,
is also coefficientwise totally positive.
(Setting
f_{n}=0
also affects rows
n
and
n+1
of
Q
,
but these are not contained in
M_{1}
.)
Then

M_{3}\;=\;[[{\bm{q}}_{0},\ldots,{\bm{q}}_{n-2},\widetilde{{\bm{q}}}_{n-1},\widetilde{{\bm{\ell}}}_{n}]]

(6.18)

is obtained from
M_{2}
by adjoining a row that has
e_{n}
in the
n
th column
and 0 elsewhere;
this is totally positive because
M_{2}
is zero beyond the
n
th column
(this reasoning is a very special case of Lemma
6.5
).
Finally, the matrix

M\;=\;[[{\bm{q}}_{0},\ldots,{\bm{q}}_{n-1},{\bm{\ell}}_{n}]]

(6.19)

can be obtained by right-multiplying
M_{3}

by the lower-bidiagonal matrix that has
1
on the diagonal,

f_{n}/e_{n}
in position
(n,n-1)
, and 0 elsewhere
(this adds
f_{n}/e_{n}
times the
n
th column to the
(n-1)
st column,
which is the content of (
6.15
)).
This completes the proof of Lemma
6.7
.

\square

Remark.
To justify the use of the quantity
f_{n}/e_{n}
,
it suffices to work in a ring of Laurent polynomials
with indeterminates
{\mathbf{e}}=(e_{n})_{n\geq 0}

and
{\mathbf{e}}^{-1}=(e_{n}^{-1})_{n\geq 0}
,
and then to consider coefficientwise total positivity in this ring.
At the end, all quantities will belong to the subring
consisting of ordinary polynomials with nonnegative exponents.

\blacksquare

Lemma 6.8
.

Fix integers
0\leq n\leq m
.
If the matrix

[[{\bm{p}}_{n+1},\ldots,{\bm{p}}_{m}]]

is coefficientwise totally positive,
then so is the matrix

M_{0}\;\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\rm def}}{{=}}\;[[{\bm{q}}_{0},\ldots,{\bm{q}}_{n-1},{\bm{\ell}}_{n},{\bm{p}}_{n+1},\ldots,{\bm{p}}_{m}]]\;.

Proof.
The case
n=m
is Lemma
6.7
; so assume that
n<m
.

Let
{\bm{t}}_{n+1}
be obtained from
{\bm{p}}_{n+1}
by specializing
d_{n}
to zero;
then

{\bm{p}}_{n+1}\;=\;{\bm{t}}_{n+1}\,+\,b_{n+1}d_{n}{\bm{\ell}}_{n}\;.

(6.20)

Note that this specialization does not affect
{\bm{p}}_{\ell}
for
\ell>n+1
.
By hypothesis

M_{1}\;\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\rm def}}{{=}}\;\begin{bmatrix}{\bm{p}}_{n+1}\\
\vdots\\
{\bm{p}}_{m}\\
\end{bmatrix}

(6.21)

is coefficientwise totally positive;
this implies, by specialization, that

M_{2}\;\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\rm def}}{{=}}\;\begin{bmatrix}{\bm{t}}_{n+1}\\
{\bm{p}}_{n+2}\\
\vdots\\
{\bm{p}}_{m}\\
\end{bmatrix}

(6.22)

is coefficientwise totally positive.
Also let

M_{3}\;\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\rm def}}{{=}}\;\begin{bmatrix}{\bm{q}}_{0}\\
\vdots\\
{\bm{q}}_{n-1}\\
{\bm{\ell}}_{n}\end{bmatrix}\;.

(6.23)

(Here for clarity we have avoided the notation
[[\;\cdots\;]]
.)

Now observe that the matrix
S\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\rm def}}{{=}}\left[\begin{array}[]{c}M_{3}\\
\hline\cr M_{2}\end{array}\right]

consists of two blocks overlapping in a single column
(namely, column
n
when the columns are numbered starting at 0):

S\;=\;\left[\begin{array}[]{c}M_{3}\\
\hline\cr M_{2}\end{array}\right]\;=\;\left[\begin{array}[]{c}{\bm{q}}_{0}\\
\vdots\\
{\bm{q}}_{n-1}\\
{\bm{\ell}}_{n}\\
\hline\cr{\bm{t}}_{n+1}\\
{\bm{p}}_{n+2}\\
\vdots\\
{\bm{p}}_{m}\end{array}\right]\;=\;\left[\begin{array}[]{c|c|c|c}*&*&*&{\bm{0}}_{n\times\infty}\\[2.84526pt]
{\bm{0}}_{1\times n-1}&f_{n}&e_{n}&{\bm{0}}_{1\times\infty}\\[2.84526pt]
\hline\cr 0&0&*&*\\[2.84526pt]
\end{array}\right]

(6.24)

where the asterisks stand for blocks of unspecified entries
(which may be zero or nonzero).
By Lemma
6.7
,
the matrix
M_{3}

is totally positive;
and we have just shown that the matrix

M_{2}

is totally positive.
So the transpose of Lemma
6.5

implies that the matrix
S
is totally positive.

On the other hand,
the matrix

M_{0}

can be obtained from
S
by left-multiplying it
by the lower-bidiagonal matrix that has
1 on the diagonal,
b_{n+1}d_{n}

in position
(n+1,n)
and zeroes elsewhere
(this adds
b_{n+1}d_{n}
times row
n
to row
n+1
,
which is the content of (
6.20
)).
This proves that the desired matrix
M_{0}
is totally positive.

\square

Lemma 6.9

(Induction step)
.

Fix integers
0\leq n\leq m
.
If the matrix

[[{\bm{q}}_{0},\ldots,{\bm{q}}_{n},{\bm{p}}_{n+1},\ldots,{\bm{p}}_{m}]]

is coefficientwise totally positive,
then so is

[[{\bm{q}}_{0},\ldots,{\bm{q}}_{n-1},{\bm{p}}_{n},\ldots,{\bm{p}}_{m}]]
.

Proof.
Notice that
{\bm{p}}_{n}={\bm{q}}_{n}+h_{n}{\bm{\ell}}_{n}
.
The matrix

[[{\bm{q}}_{0},\ldots,{\bm{q}}_{n-1},{\bm{q}}_{n},{\bm{p}}_{n+1},\ldots,{\bm{p}}_{m}]]

is totally positive by hypothesis;
and the matrix

[[{\bm{q}}_{0},\ldots,{\bm{q}}_{n-1},{\bm{\ell}}_{n},{\bm{p}}_{n+1},\ldots,{\bm{p}}_{m}]]

is totally positive by Lemma
6.8
.
So the conclusion follows by Lemma
6.3

with
a=1
and
b=h_{n}
.

\square

Lemma 6.10
.

For every pair of integers
0\leq n\leq m+1
,
the matrix

[[{\bm{q}}_{0},\ldots,{\bm{q}}_{n-1},{\bm{p}}_{n},\ldots,{\bm{p}}_{m}]]

is coefficientwise totally positive.

Proof.
Start from Lemma
6.6
,
and apply Lemma
6.9
for
n=m,m-1,\ldots,0
.

\square

This completes the proof of Theorem
6.1
.

We conclude by posing the following open problem:

Problem 6.11
.

Find a combinatorial interpretation for the output matrix
A={\mathcal{O}}(P)

generated by the production matrix
(
6.2
)/(
6.3
),
or by interesting specializations thereof.

7
The connection with multiple orthogonal polynomials

In this section we would like to explain how we were led to guess
the quadridiagonal production matrix (
1.45
),
by virtue of an unexpected connection with multiple orthogonal polynomials.

Let us first show that, for each
\alpha\geq-1
and
x\geq 0
,
the sequence
({{\mathcal{L}}_{n}^{(\alpha)}}(x))_{n\geq 0}
is a Stieltjes moment sequence,
by explicitly exhibiting its Stieltjes moment representation.
Indeed, we have the integral representation
(compare
[
77
, p. 103, eq. (5.4.1)]
)

{{\mathcal{L}}_{n}^{(\alpha)}}(x)\;=\;e^{-x}x^{-\alpha/2}\int\limits_{0}^{\infty}\!u^{n+\alpha/2}\,e^{-u}\,I_{\alpha}(2\sqrt{xu})\>du

(7.1)

valid for
\alpha>-1
, where
I_{\alpha}
is a modified Bessel function
of the first kind
[
83
, p. 77]

I_{\alpha}(z)\;=\;\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}{(z/2)^{\alpha+2k}\over k!\,\Gamma(\alpha+k+1)}\;.

(7.2)

For
n=0
, the formula (
7.1
) is easily verified
by inserting (
7.2
) and integrating term-by-term.
The general formula then follows by computing the
exponential generating function of the right-hand side
of (
7.1
),
i.e. multiplying (
7.1
) by
t^{n}/n!

and summing over
n\geq 0
;
the result is
(1-t)^{-(\alpha+1)}e^{xt/(1-t)}
,
which is indeed [by (
1.2
)]
the exponential generating function of
{{\mathcal{L}}_{n}^{(\alpha)}}(x)=n!\,L_{n}^{(\alpha)}(x)
.
And since
I_{\alpha}
is nonnegative on
[0,\infty)
,
the integral representation (
7.1
)
is a Stieltjes moment representation, with representing measure

d\mu_{\alpha,x}(u)\;=\;e^{-x}x^{-\alpha/2}\,u^{\alpha/2}\,e^{-u}\,I_{\alpha}(2\sqrt{xu})\>du\quad\hbox{ on }[0,\infty)

(7.3)

for
\alpha>-1

(with the corresponding limiting measure when
\alpha=-1
).
15
15

15

These facts were observed many years ago by Karlin

[
42
, p. 440]

[
43
, p. 62]
.

We now invoke a recently discovered
[
68
]

connection between multiple orthogonal polynomials
and branched continued fractions.
Recall that multiple orthogonal polynomials
[
40
, Chapter 23]

are a generalization of conventional orthogonal polynomials

[
77
,
15
,
40
]

in which the polynomials satisfy orthogonality relations
with respect to several measures
\mu_{1},\ldots,\mu_{r}
rather than just one.
In particular, the multiple orthogonal polynomials of type II,
denoted
P_{{\mathbf{n}}}(x)

and indexed by a multi-index
{\mathbf{n}}=(n_{1},\ldots,n_{r})\in{\mathbb{N}}^{r}
,
are monic polynomials
of degree
|{\mathbf{n}}|=\sum_{i=1}^{r}n_{i}

that satisfy the orthogonality relations

\int\!x^{k}\,P_{{\mathbf{n}}}(x)\>d\mu_{i}(x)\;=\;0\qquad\hbox{for $1\leq i\leq r$ and $0\leq k<n_{i}$}\;.

(7.4)

(Here we restrict attention to the case in which
the system of measures
\mu_{1},\ldots,\mu_{r}
is
perfect
:
namely, there exists, for each multi-index
{\mathbf{n}}
,
a
unique
monic polynomial
P_{{\mathbf{n}}}(x)

of degree
|{\mathbf{n}}|
satisfying (
7.4
).
Several general sufficient conditions for a system to be perfect are known
(Angelesco systems, AT systems, Nikishin systems, …):
see
[
40
, Chapter 23]

[
78
]
.)

Now recall

[
81
, Theorems 50.1 and 51.1]

[
79
, Chapitre V]

[
17
, Theorem 2.3 and Corollary 2.5]

[
85
, Section 5.2.1]

that the conventional orthogonal polynomials
associated to a measure
\mu
(which we here normalize to total mass 1)
satisfy a three-term recurrence relation of the form

P_{n+1}(x)\;=\;(x-\gamma_{n})\,P_{n}(x)\>-\>\beta_{n}\,P_{n-1}(x)\;,

(7.5)

where the coefficients
\gamma_{n}
and
\beta_{n}
occurring
in this recurrence relation are identical to those occurring
in the classical J-fraction for the moments of
\mu
,
i.e.

\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}a_{n}\,t^{n}\;=\;\cfrac{1}{1-\gamma_{0}t-\cfrac{\beta_{1}t^{2}}{1-\gamma_{1}t-\cfrac{\beta_{2}t^{2}}{1-\gamma_{2}t-\cfrac{\beta_{3}t^{2}}{1-\gamma_{3}t-\cdots}}}}

(7.6)

where
a_{n}=\int\!x^{n}\>d\mu(x)
.
Now, Flajolet
[
29
]
showed that the Taylor coefficient
a_{n}

in (
7.6
)
is the generating polynomial for Motzkin paths from
(0,0)
to
(n,0)

in which each rise gets weight 1,
each level step at height
i
gets weight
\gamma_{i}
,
and each fall from height
i
gets weight
\beta_{i}
.
In other words,
a_{n}
is the
(n,0)
matrix element
of the output matrix
A={\mathcal{O}}(\Pi)
,
where
\Pi
is the tridiagonal production matrix

\Pi\;=\;\begin{bmatrix}\gamma_{0}&1&&&\\
\beta_{1}&\gamma_{1}&1&&\\
&\beta_{2}&\gamma_{2}&1&\\
&&\ddots&\ddots&\ddots\end{bmatrix}\;.

(7.7)

It turns out
[
68
]

that a generalization of this connection
holds for multiple orthogonal polynomials.
Fix measures
\mu_{1},\ldots,\mu_{r}
,
and let
(P_{\mathbf{n}}(x))_{{\mathbf{n}}\in{\mathbb{N}}^{r}}

be the corresponding multiple orthogonal polynomials of type II.
Among these, let us consider the multi-indices
{\mathbf{n}}=(n_{1},\ldots,n_{r})

lying on the so-called
stepline
:
this is the near-diagonal sequence starting at
(0,0,\ldots,0)

and following the path

(n,n,\ldots,n)\to(n+1,n,\ldots,n)\to(n+1,n+1,\ldots,n)\to\ldots\to(n+1,n+1,\ldots,n+1)\to\ldots\;.

That is, we define a singly-indexed sequence
({\widetilde{P}}_{n}(x))_{n\geq 0}

by

{\widetilde{P}}_{n}(x)\;=\;P_{(n_{1},\ldots,n_{r})}(x)\quad\hbox{where }n_{i}\>=\>\Bigl\lfloor{n+r-i\over r}\Bigr\rfloor\;\;\hbox{for }1\leq i\leq r\;.

(7.8)

It is well known
[
40
, Theorem 23.1.7]

that the stepline sequence
({\widetilde{P}}_{n}(x))_{n\geq 0}

satisfies an
(r+2)
-term recurrence of the form

{\widetilde{P}}_{n+1}(x)\;=\;(x-\pi_{nn})\,{\widetilde{P}}_{n}(x)\>-\>\sum_{i=1}^{r}\pi_{n,n-i}\,{\widetilde{P}}_{n-i}(x)\;,

(7.9)

or equivalently

x{\widetilde{P}}_{n}(x)\;=\;\sum_{k=n-r}^{n+1}\pi_{nk}\,{\widetilde{P}}_{k}(x)

(7.10)

where
\pi_{n,n+1}=1
.
Here the production matrix
\Pi=(\pi_{nk})_{n,k\geq 0}

is an
(r,1)
-banded unit-lower-Hessenberg matrix,
i.e.
\pi_{nk}=0
for
k<n-r
or
k>n+1
.
Furthermore, it turns out
[
68
]

— and this is the key fact —
that the zeroth column of the output matrix
A={\mathcal{O}}(\Pi)

is precisely the moment sequence of the measure
\mu_{1}
.
Therefore, if one knows the recurrence relation
(
7.9
)/(
7.10
)
for the multiple orthogonal polynomials along the stepline,
one also knows a production matrix for
the moment sequence of the measure
\mu_{1}
.

Remark.
The subsequent columns of the output matrix
A={\mathcal{O}}(\Pi)

also have interpretations
[
68
]

as linear combinations of the moment sequences of
\mu_{1},\ldots,\mu_{r}
.
For instance, for
0\leq i\leq r-1
,
the
i
th column of the output matrix
is that particular linear combination of the moment sequences
of
\mu_{1},\mu_{2},\ldots,\mu_{i+1}

that annihilates the first
i
entries
and makes the next entry equal to 1.
But we will not need this fact here.

\blacksquare

Return now to our Laguerre polynomials
and their Stieltjes moment representation
with the measure
\mu_{\alpha,x}
defined in (
7.3
).
We then benefit from exceedingly good fortune:
some years ago, Coussement and Van Assche
[
19
]

studied the multiple orthogonal polynomials with
r=2

associated to the pair of measures

(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})=(\mu_{\alpha,x},\mu_{\alpha+1,x})
.
(They actually used a slightly different normalization,
so that their moments are a rescaled version of the
reversed monic unsigned Laguerre polynomials
{{\overline{\mathcal{L}}}_{n}^{(\alpha)}}(x)
:
compare
[
19
, Lemma 1]
to our (
1.1
).)
And they computed explicitly, among other things,
the four-term recurrence relation for the multiple orthogonal polynomials
of type II along the stepline
[
19
, Theorem 9]
.
After translating their normalization to ours,
this four-term recurrence corresponds precisely to the
quadridiagonal production matrix (
1.45
)
for the monic unsigned Laguerre polynomials
{{\mathcal{L}}_{n}^{(\alpha)}}(x)
.
This is how we first discovered these production matrices.

From here it was a small step to discover the production matrix
(
1.47
)
for our multivariate Laguerre polynomials.
Indeed, once we had exploited the Foata–Strehl
[
31
]

combinatorial model to define the multivariate Laguerre polynomials
(
1.41
),
it was not difficult to guess
— helped by a bit of computer experimentation —
how the production matrix (
1.45
),
with its single variable
x
, should be refined to introduce
the further variables
{y_{\rm p}},{y_{\rm v}},{y_{\rm da}},{y_{\rm dd}},{y_{\rm fp}}
.

Let us also observe that there is another way that we could have
discovered the production matrix
(
1.45
)
for the univariate Laguerre polynomials.
As observed preceding Proposition
3.1
,
we already knew a tridiagonal production matrix for
the Laguerre polynomials at
x=0
,
which are
{{\mathcal{L}}_{n}^{(\alpha)}}(0)=(1+\alpha)^{\overline{n}}
.
It would then have been natural to try perturbing this tridiagonal matrix
by terms linear in
x
; and if one also realized that one ought to consider
introducing a second subdiagonal, one could try the Ansatz

p_{n,n+1}&=1\\
p_{n,n}=(2n+1+\alpha)\,+\,A_{n}x\\
p_{n,n-1}=n(n+\alpha)\,+\,B_{n}x\\
p_{n,n-2}=C_{n}x\\
p_{n,k}=0\qquad\textrm{if $k<n-2$ or $k>n+1$}

(7.11)

A little computer work
— setting the zeroth column of the output matrix equal to
{{\mathcal{L}}_{n}^{(\alpha)}}(x)
—
would then lead quickly to the values of
A_{n},B_{n},C_{n}
for small
n
,
from which one could easily guess that
A_{n}=1
,
B_{n}=2n
and
C_{n}=n(n-1)
.
This is not, in fact, the way we first found the production matrix
(
1.45
),
but it easily could have been.

Appendix A
Generalized and modified
\bm{m}
-Stieltjes–Rogers polynomials

In this Appendix we introduce, for each integer
m\geq 1
,
an infinite sequence of triangular arrays, indexed by an integer
j\geq 0
,
whose matrix elements we call
the
generalized
m
-Stieltjes–Rogers polynomials of type
j
.
We examine in particular the entries in these matrices’ zeroth columns,
which we call the

modified
m
-Stieltjes–Rogers polynomials of type
j
.
When
j=0
these polynomials
reduce to the (generalized)
m
-Stieltjes–Rogers polynomials
introduced in
[
59
, sections 5, 7 and 9]
.
When
m=1
they reduce to the generalized
classical Stieltjes–Rogers polynomials
of the first (
j=0
) and second (
j=1
) kinds.
The discussion here expands and supersedes the treatment
in
[
59
]
.

We begin (Section
A.1
)
by reviewing the the theory of classical Stieltjes–Rogers polynomials,
and then (Section
A.2
)
the basic ideas from
[
59
]

concerning the
m
-Stieltjes–Rogers polynomials (of type 0).
Then (Section
A.3
) we introduce the
generalized
m
-Stieltjes–Rogers polynomials of type
j
.
Finally (Section
A.4
),
we apply this theory to the univariate Laguerre production matrix
(
1.45
).

A.1
Classical Stieltjes–Rogers polynomials

A
Dyck path
is a path in the upper half-plane
{\mathbb{Z}}\times{\mathbb{N}}
,
starting and ending on the horizontal axis,
using steps
(1,1)
[“rise” or “up step”]
and
(1,-1)
[“fall” or “down step”].
More generally, a
Dyck path at level
k

is a path in
{\mathbb{Z}}\times{\mathbb{N}}_{\geq k}
,
starting and ending at height
k
,
using steps
(1,1)
and
(1,-1)
.
Clearly a Dyck path must be of even length;
we denote by
{\mathcal{D}}_{2n}
the set of Dyck paths from
(0,0)
to
(2n,0)
.

Now let
{\bm{\alpha}}=(\alpha_{i})_{i\geq 1}
be an infinite set of indeterminates,
and let
S_{n}({\bm{\alpha}})
be the generating polynomial
for Dyck paths from
(0,0)
to
(2n,0)
in which each rise gets weight 1
and each fall from height
i
gets weight
\alpha_{i}
.
Clearly
S_{n}({\bm{\alpha}})
is a homogeneous polynomial
of degree
n
with nonnegative integer coefficients;
following Flajolet
[
29
]
,
we call it the
Stieltjes–Rogers polynomial
of order
n
.

Let
f_{0}(t)=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}S_{n}({\bm{\alpha}})\,t^{n}

be the ordinary generating function for Dyck paths with these weights
(considered as a formal power series in
t
);
and more generally, let
f_{k}(t)
be the ordinary generating function
for Dyck paths at level
k
with these same weights.
(Obviously
f_{k}
is just
f_{0}
with each
\alpha_{i}
replaced by
\alpha_{i+k}
;
but we shall not explicitly use this fact.)
Then a straightforward “renewal” argument
[
29
]

gives the functional equation

f_{k}(t)\;=\;1\>+\>\alpha_{k+1}t\,f_{k}(t)\,f_{k+1}(t)

(A.1)

or equivalently

f_{k}(t)\;=\;{1\over 1\>-\>\alpha_{k+1}t\,f_{k+1}(t)}\;.

(A.2)

Iterating (
A.2
), we see immediately that
f_{k}

is given by the continued fraction

f_{k}(t)\;=\;\cfrac{1}{1-\cfrac{\alpha_{k+1}t}{1-\cfrac{\alpha_{k+2}t}{1-\cfrac{\alpha_{k+3}t}{1-\cdots}}}}

(A.3)

and in particular that
f_{0}
is given by

f_{0}(t)\;=\;\cfrac{1}{1-\cfrac{\alpha_{1}t}{1-\cfrac{\alpha_{2}t}{1-\cfrac{\alpha_{3}t}{1-\cdots}}}}\;\,.

(A.4)

The right-hand sides of (
A.3
)/(
A.4
)
are called (classical)
Stieltjes-type continued fractions
,
or (classical)
S-fractions
for short.
This combinatorial interpretation of S-fractions
in terms of weighted Dyck paths is due to Flajolet
[
29
]
.

We now generalize the Stieltjes–Rogers polynomials to a triangular array,
as follows:
We use the term
partial Dyck path

to denote a path in the upper half-plane
{\mathbb{Z}}\times{\mathbb{N}}
,
using steps
(1,1)
and
(1,-1)
,
that starts on the horizontal axis
but is allowed to end anywhere in the upper half-plane.
For
n,\ell\geq 0
, we define the

generalized Stieltjes–Rogers polynomial of the first kind

S_{n,\ell}({\bm{\alpha}})

to be the generating polynomial for partial Dyck paths
starting at
(0,0)
and ending at
(2n,2\ell)
,
in which each rise gets weight 1
and each fall from height
i
gets weight
\alpha_{i}
.
Obviously
S_{n,\ell}
is nonvanishing only for
0\leq\ell\leq n
,
so we have an infinite lower-triangular array

{\sf S}=(S_{n,\ell}({\bm{\alpha}}))_{n,\ell\geq 0}

in which the zeroth column displays
the ordinary Stieltjes–Rogers polynomials
S_{n,0}=S_{n}
.
In particular we have
S_{n,n}=1

and
S_{n,n-1}=\sum_{i=1}^{2n-1}\alpha_{i}
.

Analogously, we define
the
generalized Stieltjes–Rogers polynomial of the second kind

S^{\prime}_{n,\ell}({\bm{\alpha}})

to be the generating polynomial for partial Dyck paths
starting at
(0,0)
and ending at
(2n+1,2\ell+1)
,
in which again each rise gets weight 1
and each fall from height
i
gets weight
\alpha_{i}
.
Since
S^{\prime}_{n,\ell}
is nonvanishing only for
0\leq\ell\leq n
,
we obtain a second infinite lower-triangular array

{\sf S}^{\prime}=(S^{\prime}_{n,\ell}({\bm{\alpha}}))_{n,\ell\geq 0}
.
In particular we have
S^{\prime}_{n,n}=1

and
S^{\prime}_{n,n-1}=\sum_{i=1}^{2n}\alpha_{i}
.

The polynomials
S_{n,\ell}({\bm{\alpha}})
and
S^{\prime}_{n,\ell}({\bm{\alpha}})

manifestly satisfy the joint recurrence

S^{\prime}_{n,\ell}&=S_{n,\ell}\>+\>\alpha_{2\ell+2}\,S_{n,\ell+1}\\
S_{n+1,\ell}=S^{\prime}_{n,\ell-1}\>+\>\alpha_{2\ell+1}\,S^{\prime}_{n,\ell}{}

(A.5)

for
n,\ell\geq 0
,
with the initial condition
S_{0,\ell}=\delta_{\ell 0}

(where of course we also set
{S^{\prime}_{n,-1}=0}
).
It follows that the
S_{n,\ell}
satisfy the recurrence

S_{n+1,\ell}\;=\;S_{n,\ell-1}\>+\>(\alpha_{2\ell}+\alpha_{2\ell+1})\,S_{n,\ell}\>+\>\alpha_{2\ell+1}\alpha_{2\ell+2}\,S_{n,\ell+1}

(A.6)

with the initial condition
S_{0,\ell}=\delta_{\ell 0}

(where we set
S_{n,-1}=0
and
\alpha_{0}=0
).
In other words, the unit-lower-triangular array
{\sf S}

has the tridiagonal production matrix

P\;=\;\begin{bmatrix}\alpha_{1}&1&&&\\
\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2}&\alpha_{2}+\alpha_{3}&1&&\\
&\alpha_{3}\alpha_{4}&\alpha_{4}+\alpha_{5}&1&\\
&&\ddots&\ddots&\ddots\end{bmatrix}\;.

(A.7)

Please observe now that the production matrix (
A.7
)
can be factorized as a product of two bidiagonal matrices:

\begin{bmatrix}\alpha_{1}&1&&&\\
\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2}&\alpha_{2}+\alpha_{3}&1&&\\
&\alpha_{3}\alpha_{4}&\alpha_{4}+\alpha_{5}&1&\\
&&\ddots&\ddots&\ddots\end{bmatrix}\;=\;\begin{bmatrix}1&&&&\\
\alpha_{2}&1&&&\\
&\alpha_{4}&1&&\\
&&\ddots&\ddots&\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}\alpha_{1}&1&&&\\
&\alpha_{3}&1&&\\
&&\alpha_{5}&1&\\
&&&\ddots&\ddots\end{bmatrix}

(A.8)

By Lemma
2.1
this shows
[
82
, Lemma 3.3]

that the production matrix (
A.7
)
is totally positive in the ring
Z[{\bm{\alpha}}]
equipped with the
coefficientwise order.
It then follows
[
70
]

from Theorems
2.9
and
2.15

that the unit-lower-triangular array
{\sf S}
of
generalized Stieltjes–Rogers polynomials
is coefficientwise totally positive,
and that the sequence
(S_{n}({\bm{\alpha}}))_{n\geq 0}

of Stieltjes–Rogers polynomials is coefficientwise Hankel-totally positive.

Similar considerations apply to the matrix
{\sf S}^{\prime}

of generalized Stieltjes–Rogers polynomials of the second kind

[
59
, eq. (7.12)]
;
we leave the details as an exercise for the reader.

A.2
\bm{m}
-Stieltjes–Rogers polynomials (branched S-fractions)

In what follows we fix an integer
m\geq 1
.
We recall
[
4
,
10
,
62
,
59
]

that an
\bm{m}
-Dyck path

is a path in the upper half-plane
{\mathbb{Z}}\times{\mathbb{N}}
,
starting and ending on the horizontal axis,
using steps
(1,1)
[“rise” or “up step”]
and
(1,-m)
[“
m
-fall” or “down step”].
More generally, an
\bm{m}
-Dyck path at level
\bm{k}

is a path in
{\mathbb{Z}}\times{\mathbb{N}}_{\geq k}
,
starting and ending at height
k
,
using steps
(1,1)
and
(1,-m)
.
Since the number of up steps must equal
m
times the number of down steps,
the length of an
m
-Dyck path must be a multiple of
m+1
.

Now let
{\bm{\alpha}}=(\alpha_{i})_{i\geq m}
be an infinite set of indeterminates.
Then
[
59
]

the
\bm{m}
-Stieltjes–Rogers polynomial
of order
n
,
denoted
S^{(m)}_{n}({\bm{\alpha}})
, is the generating polynomial
for
m
-Dyck paths from
(0,0)
to
((m+1)n,0)

in which each rise gets weight 1
and each
m
-fall from height
i
gets weight
\alpha_{i}
.
Clearly
S_{n}^{(m)}({\bm{\alpha}})
is a homogeneous polynomial
of degree
n
with nonnegative integer coefficients.

Let
f_{0}(t)=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}S^{(m)}_{n}({\bm{\alpha}})\,t^{n}

be the ordinary generating function for
m
-Dyck paths with these weights;
and more generally, let
f_{k}(t)
be the ordinary generating function
for
m
-Dyck paths at level
k
with these same weights.
(Obviously
f_{k}
is just
f_{0}
with each
\alpha_{i}
replaced by
\alpha_{i+k}
;
but we shall not explicitly use this fact.)
Then straightforward combinatorial arguments
[
59
]

lead to the functional equation

f_{k}(t)\;=\;1\>+\>\alpha_{k+m}t\,f_{k}(t)\,f_{k+1}(t)\,\cdots\,f_{k+m}(t)

(A.9)

or equivalently

f_{k}(t)\;=\;{1\over 1\>-\>\alpha_{k+m}t\,f_{k+1}(t)\,\cdots\,f_{k+m}(t)}\;.

(A.10)

Iterating (
A.10
), we see immediately that
f_{k}

is given by the branched continued fraction
